# SAND-T1 — the table

Synthetic content: invented characters in an invented campaign (Table C's Beregost files, docs/campaigns/baldurs-gate-en). Stage origin/claude/handoff at 23220d6 (docs/stages/GAME_PARTY_1.md, the amendment of 2026-10-27 16:36 LA); design sha256 `e46731065b41ce8f0c70dccdbfc5309c86c2a4fb68fb452b4170c3ed8fb7d871`.

Arm **P-SAND**, triad T1; 25 round(s) of 25; ended by rounds. The DM: Claude Opus 5.5 with the owner's settings (Opus writes — Opus records, depth high → xhigh); the relay: the same model at medium. Started 2026-10-28T02:16:16.267Z.

Seats: 1. Bram Halloway (fighter) — Claude Opus 5.5; 2. Sefa Marrin (warlock) — GPT-6.1 Sol; 3. Pell Dunmore (rogue) — DeepSeek V4 Pro 0813. The players are not told what the others are.

Known condition: Seat 1's character (Bram) is the app's hero record; Sefa and Pell are party companions with full records. Known condition, not corrected: the DM's frame is written around the hero and may give that seat more attention; Heroic Inspiration is the hero's only; the allowed rerolls (2 per session) are the table's, shared by all three. The rotation puts each family in that seat once per triad.

**No hidden layer:** P-SAND plays pure simulation (no prep).

The world's snapshot: sha256 `1649b61c08e027ff44de2bb5065046f872e383a00c5d04803d95553f58568f5a` (the same wizard, sheets and opening for both triads).

## The opening

**The DM's opening (as committed by the wizard):**

````text
Beregost smells of coal smoke and hot iron even in here. Through the shutters comes the steady ring of hammers from the smithies along the Coast Way, and the light is going amber. The Burning Wizard's common room is filling up for the evening: carters, a pair of dusty sellswords, townsfolk with soot still on their forearms. The benches are crowded, and that is how three strangers end up at the same table.

Bram Halloway sits with his back to the wall and his shield propped against the bench, the old company mark painted over. Across from him is Sefa Marrin, a young woman just off the road from Baldur's Gate. Beside her is Pell Dunmore, a lean man from the Lower City with a healer's satchel slung at his hip. He came in by the western road and sat down so quietly that nobody heard him do it.

None of them has said a word to the others yet. Then Orla Tarnwick, the innkeeper, pushes through the crowd with a jug in one hand and three cups hooked on her fingers. She sets them down hard and looks from one face to the next.

"Last free seats in the house, and you three took them together," she says. "So: one tab or three? And what brings each of you into Beregost tonight?"
````

## Round 1

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 1 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram glances up at the innkeeper, then at the two strangers. He nods to each of them, the way you'd nod to someone sharing a campfire.

"Three tabs. I don't know these two from Tymora, and they don't know me." He sets a few silver on the table for his own cup. "Ale, if it's ale in that jug. And a bowl of whatever's in the pot."

He leans back against the wall and keeps his shield close. "As for what brings me: my last caravan paid me off in Nashkel. Sixteen years walking the Way, and for once nobody's told me where to walk next. Beregost seemed as good a place as any to sit still and think about it."

His eyes move over the room while he talks. The sellswords, the carters, the door. It's an old habit. Then he looks back at the young woman and the man with the satchel.

"You two look like the road brought you in too. Which way'd you come?"

Sefa Marrin: “Three tabs, please. We haven’t had time to become bad influences on one another.”

I settle my pack beneath the bench. “Something hot to eat, and a small ale. What would a bed cost for tonight?”

At her other question, I rub a little road dust from my sleeve. “I’ve come down from the Gate. No appointment waiting here, for once. Supper seemed a good place to start.”

I turn to include both strangers. “Sefa Marrin.” My glance pauses on the healer’s satchel. “I used to work in a hospice. Are you travelling, or do you practise here?”

Pell Dunmore: Pell turns his cup the right way up without looking at it, and says, flat but not unfriendly: "Three. I pay my own."

When the innkeeper's eyes settle on him for the second question, he gives a little shrug, one shoulder. "Passing through. I'm for hire, if there's work worth the road—caravans, lost property, the usual." He lets his gaze drift back toward the room as he says it, letting the answer be true without being much of an answer. "A bed and dinner'll do for tonight."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla doesn't write anything down. She looks at each of them once, and you can tell she'll remember the three tabs better than most clerks would.

"Three it is." She pours Bram's cup first. The ale is brown and a little cloudy, and it comes out of the jug with a proper head. "Ale it is. The pot's mutton and barley, and it's been on since noon, so it's soft. Four coppers the cup, six the bowl." She slides one of his silver toward her apron pocket and pushes the rest back across the board with two fingers. "There's your change. Don't leave it lying there in here."

She pours a smaller measure for Sefa without being asked twice. "Beds are upstairs. Five silver gets you a cot in the long room. There's a curtain, and the carters snore. Eight gets you a room with a door and a bolt, but I've only got two of those left tonight, and the night's young." She tips her head at Pell. "Same for you. Supper and a bed, I heard. Tell me which bed before I've let them both."

Pell's cup gets filled last. Her eyes drop for a moment to the satchel on his hip, then go back to Bram.

"Nashkel," she says. She's stopped hurrying. "You came up the Way, then. Today?" She hooks the empty jug on one finger. "Because the last two lots that came up from Nashkel came up short. The Halvering wagons never came in at all. I had a room held for their factor nine days, and in the end I let it." She says it flatly, the way you report weather that has already done its damage. "So if you saw anything on the road, there's people in this room who'd buy you the next cup to hear it."

At the next table, one of the two dusty sellswords has stopped talking to his partner. He's a heavy man with a sunburnt neck, and he's looking at Bram's shield, at the brown paint over the old company mark. He doesn't look away when Bram's eyes come round to him. He just lifts his own cup a little, as if he's waiting to hear the answer as well.

Orla is already half turned toward the kitchen. She's still waiting, though, for Bram's answer about the road and for the other two to pick their beds. Sefa's question is still hanging across the table as well, and so is Bram's: the man with the satchel hasn't said which road brought him.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore]

## Round 2

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 2 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram sweeps the change into his palm and pockets it without counting. He nods at Orla's warning.

"Room with a door, if one's still going. Eight silver." He sets them on the board. "I've slept next to enough carters to last me."

Then he turns the cup slowly in his hand and thinks before he answers.

"Left Nashkel four days back. Our wagons came up whole, but we had twelve guards for six wagons and the factor was paying for every one of them. That tells you something by itself." He takes a drink. "Road was quiet. Too quiet past the Lionshead crossing, if you want my honest feeling. No traders coming the other way for most of a day. And in Nashkel the talk was all of wagons that left and never got anywhere. Nobody I'd call a reliable mouth, though."

He looks over at the heavy sellsword with the sunburnt neck and meets his gaze evenly. He lifts his own cup back the same small amount.

"You've got a question, friend, you can ask it. The paint's just paint. The company it covered is mostly dead." His tone stays even, without any edge to it. "Bram Halloway."

He turns back to the table and gives Sefa a short nod for her name, then looks at the man with the satchel.

"Hospice and a healer's bag at one table. If anything goes wrong in here tonight, I'll know which way to fall." There's a dry glint in his eye. "You never said which road brought you in."

Sefa Marrin: “A room with a bolt, please.” I count out nine silver for the room, supper and ale. “Best buy one before the snoring starts.”

To the shield man, I say, “From Baldur’s Gate, south down the Way. Nothing worse than sore feet on my stretch.”

I leave him room to answer Orla, watching the exchange without turning to stare at the next table. When there’s a pause, I ask her, “When you say the others came up short—missing wagons, or missing people? And has anyone come back with word of the Halverings?”

Pell Dunmore: "High Hedge road," Pell says to the shield man, not making much of it. "West. Took the long way round because the day was fine." Then he nods at Sefa's satchel guess. "It's my trade, not my calling. I do courier work when the message has to arrive wet."

He shifts half an inch on the bench, so the door and the shield man's wall are both in the corner of his eye. To Orla: "Cot'll do me. I sleep light, and the snoring tells me no one's died in the night."

He raises the cup but doesn't drink yet. "Hired blades and folk just off the road all in one pot. Tell me when the singing starts, so I can see it coming." A beat, then quieter, to nobody: "Or leave before it does."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla takes Bram's eight silver and Sefa's nine with one sweep of the hand and doesn't count either pile. She can see they're right.

"That's both rooms gone," she says. "Top of the stair. Yours is on the left, the lady's on the right. Bolts work. Don't lean on the shutters." She looks at Pell. "Cot, then. Third from the window, the snoring's thinner there. Five for the cot, six with your supper. Pay me now or at breakfast, I'll know either way."

Pell's line about the singing gets a short look from her. "There's no singing. The last bard who started up got paid to stop. Best coin I ever spent."

She turns to Sefa's question and answers it straight. "Both. Wagons, and the people with them. The first lot came in two wagons short, and the drivers went with the wagons. Nobody's come back from the Halverings. Their house in the Gate wrote last week asking where their factor had got to." She tucks the coins into her apron. "Wish I knew. I held his room."

At the next table the heavy man sets his cup down. Up close the sunburn runs down into his collar. He has a broad, flat face, and his knuckles are scarred white across the tops. His partner is a lean, younger woman with her hair cropped like a soldier's. She hasn't touched her drink, and she keeps her eyes on Bram's hands rather than his face.

"Tam Brodder," the big man says. His voice is slow and low, and he gives every word its own space. "Wasn't the paint. Saw how you sat down. Back to the wall, shield where your hand falls on it." He jerks a thumb sideways. "Ilse Var."

The woman nods once and says nothing.

"I was on the second lot," Tam says. "Ambrel's. Eleven days back. Five wagons out of Nashkel, four into Beregost." He turns the cup a quarter turn on the board. "Fourth night we camped at the old waystone, south of the crossing. In the morning the tail wagon was gone. Mules, driver, load, all of it. Dogs never barked." He waits a moment. "I had the watch."

Ilse looks at him then, briefly, and back at Bram.

"Ruts ran off the road east, toward the hills. A hundred feet in they hit shale and stopped. Like it lifted off." His jaw works. "Ambrel says it's paid for and done, so we're done." He looks at Bram from under heavy brows. "You said quiet past the crossing. Quiet how? Did you see anyone on that road who wasn't trading?"
````

The shield man sweeps his change into his palm and pockets it without counting, and nods at Orla's warning. "Room with a door, if one's still going. Eight silver." He sets them on the board. "I've slept next to enough carters to last me." He turns his cup slowly before he goes on. "Left Nashkel four days back. Our wagons came up whole, but we had twelve guards for six wagons and the factor was paying for every one of them. That tells you something by itself." He drinks. "Road was quiet. Too quiet past the Lionshead crossing, if you want my honest feeling. No traders coming the other way for most of a day. And in Nashkel the talk was all of wagons that left and never got anywhere. Nobody I'd call a reliable mouth, though."

He looks at the heavy sellsword with the sunburnt neck at the next table, meets his gaze evenly, and lifts his own cup back the same small amount. "You've got a question, friend, you can ask it. The paint's just paint. The company it covered is mostly dead." His tone stays even. "Bram Halloway."

He turns back and gives you a short nod for your name, then looks at the man with the satchel. "Hospice and a healer's bag at one table. If anything goes wrong in here tonight, I'll know which way to fall." There's a dry glint in his eye. "You never said which road brought you in."

The man with the satchel answers without making much of it. "High Hedge road. West. Took the long way round because the day was fine." He nods at you for the satchel guess. "It's my trade, not my calling. I do courier work when the message has to arrive wet." He shifts half an inch on the bench. To Orla: "Cot'll do me. I sleep light, and the snoring tells me no one's died in the night." He raises his cup but doesn't drink yet. "Hired blades and folk just off the road all in one pot. Tell me when the singing starts, so I can see it coming." A beat, then quieter, to nobody: "Or leave before it does."

Orla takes Bram's eight silver and your nine in one sweep of the hand and doesn't count either pile. She can see they're right.

"That's both rooms gone," she says. "Top of the stair. His is on the left, yours is on the right. Bolts work. Don't lean on the shutters." She looks at the man with the satchel. "Cot, then. Third from the window, the snoring's thinner there. Five for the cot, six with your supper. Pay me now or at breakfast, I'll know either way."

His line about the singing gets a short look from her. "There's no singing. The last bard who started up got paid to stop. Best coin I ever spent."

She answers your question straight. "Both. Wagons, and the people with them. The first lot came in two wagons short, and the drivers went with the wagons. Nobody's come back from the Halverings. Their house in the Gate wrote last week asking where their factor had got to." She tucks the coins into her apron. "Wish I knew. I held his room."

At the next table the heavy man sets his cup down. Up close the sunburn runs down into his collar. He has a broad, flat face, and his knuckles are scarred white across the tops. His partner is a lean, younger woman with her hair cropped like a soldier's. She hasn't touched her drink, and she keeps her eyes on Bram's hands rather than his face.

"Tam Brodder," the big man says. His voice is slow and low, and he gives every word its own space. "Wasn't the paint. Saw how you sat down. Back to the wall, shield where your hand falls on it." He jerks a thumb sideways. "Ilse Var."

The woman nods once and says nothing.

"I was on the second lot," Tam says. "Ambrel's. Eleven days back. Five wagons out of Nashkel, four into Beregost." He turns the cup a quarter turn on the board. "Fourth night we camped at the old waystone, south of the crossing. In the morning the tail wagon was gone. Mules, driver, load, all of it. Dogs never barked." He waits a moment. "I had the watch."

Ilse looks at him then, briefly, and back at Bram.

"Ruts ran off the road east, toward the hills. A hundred feet in they hit shale and stopped. Like it lifted off." His jaw works. "Ambrel says it's paid for and done, so we're done." He looks at Bram from under heavy brows. "You said quiet past the crossing. Quiet how? Did you see anyone on that road who wasn't trading?"
````

## Round 3

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 3 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't answer straight away. He sets the cup down and looks at Tam the way he'd look at a man reporting in at the end of a watch: level and without judgment.

"Had the watch and the dogs never barked," he says quietly. "I've stood that watch. Not that one, but one like it. It sits with you."

He thinks back, frowning a little.

"Quiet how... No traders coming south for most of a day, like I said. That's wrong for this time of year. Should've been a dozen carts heading down to the Nashkel fair." He rubs his thumb along the rim of the cup. "We passed the waystone midday, didn't camp there. Factor wanted to push on to the crossing. I won't swear I saw anyone. But there were fresh fire-scars off the road on the hill side, two or three nights old, and no tracks coming back down to the Way from them. I took it for drovers. Now I'm less sure."

He leans forward on his forearms and drops his voice so it stays at the two tables.

"Ruts that go a hundred feet and stop on shale didn't fly anywhere. Somebody took that wagon across rock on purpose, someone who knows how to hide a trail. And dogs that don't bark either knew the people, or couldn't." He glances at Ilse, then back at Tam. "The driver. Was he new, or was he an Ambrel man from way back? Did the mules go quiet too? And did anybody look past the shale, or did Ambrel just count his loss and roll on?"

He sits back against the wall.

"I'm not hired by anybody right now, so I'm not selling you anything. But the Halvering factor's nine days gone, and four wagons out of five is a pattern, not bad luck." He tips his head at Sefa and the man with the satchel. "Seems nobody at this table has anywhere to be. I'd hear the rest of it, if you'll tell it."

Sefa Marrin: I set my cup down. “That’s a hard thing to have happen on your watch.”

I wait for Bram’s answer, then turn a little toward Tam so he needn’t speak across the room.

“Sefa Marrin. May I ask—did you lose any time that night? A stretch you couldn’t account for, or someone having to wake you? I’m not asking to find fault. A whole wagon leaving quietly, with the dogs asleep, makes me wonder whether someone helped keep the camp quiet.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell finally drinks, a short pull, and sets the cup down with the handle turned toward his knife hand.

"High Hedge, west," he says to Bram, with the patience of a man repeating a thing that didn't need repeating. "I came around by the old coast track, not the Way. So I've seen nothing on your road except this room's opinion of it."

He lets the mutter of talk settle, then says, quieter, to the table rather than to any one of them: "Missing wagons. That's two tells. Somebody's either bold and well-horsed enough to lift goods off the trade road—or there's a reason the dogs never bark and a wheel-track stops like a thing grew wings."

His gaze flicks to Tam, then Ilse, not unkind. "Ambrel paid you off. That means the story's closed for Ambrel, but it's not closed for the next factor with five wagons and four guards figuring he'll risk it." He turns his cup an inch. "Somebody's going to be hired to find out which it is. Question is whether it's us, or somebody with less sense."

Pell looks at the sellswords and then at Bram and Sefa, reading the table like a man counting coins before he'll say what he'll bet. "I don't walk the road for the exercise. But I can track coin, doors and people, and if there's a factor or a guild in here tonight with gold and no good answer yet, I'd hear their offer." He lets that hang, then finishes: "Better than waiting for the next wagon to go missing and having to explain why I sat in an inn through it."

He gives Orla a small nod, not unfriendly. "Five for the cot. I'll pay at breakfast for the supper, same as the ale. I haven't decided if I'm staying long enough to be charged for it."

Outside, a cart rattles past, and the hammer-song from the smithies has dropped to a lower, slower rhythm as the light thins. Pell's eyes do one slow pass over the room—door, window, the stairs, the sellswords' table—and then settle back on his cup.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam listens to all of it without moving. When Bram gets to the fire-scars, his eyes narrow.

"Two or three nights old," he says. "And you came by four days back. So somebody was up on that hill a week ago. That's after mine." He lets it sit for a moment. "Driver was Corrin Dace. Six years with Ambrel. Wife in Nashkel. He wasn't new." He takes a slow breath through his nose. "Mules neither. Two of them. A mule'll bray at a fox, Halloway. Nothing brayed."

He looks at Sefa next. Her question seems to cost him something, but he answers it straight.

"No. I walked the line every hour. Didn't sit." He stops for a moment. "Fire burned down faster than it ought to. Third hour, I was putting wood on and thought I'd let it go. Thought it was me."

"I was asleep by that fire," Ilse says. Her eyes are on Bram's hands. "Didn't wake."

"Past the shale," Tam goes on. "Ilse and me walked it a couple hundred feet. Rock, then scrub, then more rock. Ambrel gave us till the sun was over the hill. Then we rolled on." He sets his big scarred hands flat on the board. "He's done with it. His money's spent. Mine isn't."

Orla is back by now with three bowls of mutton and barley on a board, steaming, and she sets one in front of each of them. She was close enough to hear Pell. She takes the five silver he puts down and taps the board once in front of him. "One silver at breakfast, then. Supper and the ale."

"You asked who's got gold and no answer," she says to him. Her voice is as flat as it was for the prices. "Nobody in this room. The Halvering house wrote to me. I've got the letter behind the counter. They're asking after their factor, not hiring anybody, but people who write letters about a man usually pay for news of him." She tucks the board under her arm. "I'm not their clerk. Ask me for it and you can read it."

Then she's gone toward the kitchen.

Tam watches her go and turns back to the table.

"Ilse and me are riding back down to the waystone. Day after tomorrow, when the horses are rested," he says. "We'll look past the shale properly this time, and up that hill of yours." He looks at Bram, then at the other two, one after another, and takes his time over each face. "I can't pay you. But I'd take company that can sit a watch."

 (Out of game: no roll was needed. Sefa's question and setting down her cup are just conversation, and Tam answers her in the scene.)
````

## Round 4

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 4 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram pulls the bowl toward him but doesn't pick up the spoon yet. He's watching Tam's hands, flat on the board.

"A fire that burns low, a watchman who thinks it's his own fault, a soldier who sleeps through it and dogs and mules that make no sound." He says it slowly, as if he's laying out stones. "I'm no hedge-wizard, Brodder, but I've heard old hands talk. That's not tiredness, and it wasn't you. Somebody put that camp to sleep. Somebody who knew how, and who had the coin or the gift for it." He picks up the spoon at last and points it at Tam. "Stop carrying that watch like you dropped it. Somebody took it out of your hands."

He eats a mouthful, chews, and nods once, as though he's settled something.

"Day after tomorrow suits me. I'll sit a watch. Two, if we're short." His eyes go to Ilse. "And this time nobody sleeps alone by the fire. We stand watch in pairs, back to back, and we keep each other talking. If one of us goes quiet, the other kicks him."

Then he looks across the table at Sefa and the man with the satchel. There's no pressure in it.

"That's my road decided, it seems. You two owe nobody anything. But if you're of a mind, a hospice hand and a man who carries a healer's bag wouldn't go amiss where we're going." A dry beat. "You never gave a name, by the way."

He raises his voice a little toward the kitchen as Orla goes past.

"Mistress, I'll read that letter, if the offer stands. After supper's fine." He adds more quietly to the table, "If the Halverings are asking, they might know what their factor was carrying. A man takes a wagon off the road for its load. I'd like to know what the load was."

Sefa Marrin: “I can sit a watch. And if Corrin’s still alive, I may be more use when you find him.”

I draw my bowl closer, but leave the spoon alone for a moment. “I’ll come. I haven’t a horse, though. We’ll need to settle that before you leave.”

Then I glance toward the counter. “I’d like to read that letter, too. See who the Halverings are missing, and where to send word.”

To Tam, I add, “Did the fire burn the wood away unusually fast, or did it stop giving light and heat with wood still on it? Those are different troubles.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell eats a few spoonfuls before he answers, slow enough that it's clear he's thinking and not just being coy.

"Day after tomorrow," he repeats. "I can sit a watch, and I can follow sign when it's too dark for most. I'm not a caravan guard, but if the road's got something on it that lifts wagons, I'd rather see it from the hill than from the back of one."

He glances toward the counter, where the Halvering letter is, then back to Tam. "I'll go. For a fair share of whatever the road turns up—if it turns up anything. If it's just weather and a bad wheel and a nervous factor, then we've all spent a day for nothing, and I'll still have had the ride."

He wipes the spoon on the edge of the bowl and says, to Bram and Sefa as much as to the sellswords: "One rule, if I'm the one on watch: when I wave you down, you get down. I've seen too many good ambushes turn into fair fights because somebody stood up to ask why."

He tips his head at Orla as she moves off. "And I'd read the letter before we ride. A man whose wagon disappears and whose house still writes after him—worth knowing what they feared before they lost him."

Pell lets that hang a moment, then eats again, his back to the wall and his eyes on the door without seeming to be.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam doesn't move while Bram talks. When the spoon points at him, his eyes drop to it and come back up. For a moment he looks like a man who's been handed something and doesn't know where to put it down.

"Maybe," he says. A long breath. "Still had it, Halloway. Whoever took it."

That's all he says about it. Ilse is the one who answers the part about the watch. She glances at Bram's spoon hand, then at his face, which is rare for her.

"Pairs. Good." She waits a moment. "I'll take the third hour."

Tam turns to Sefa's question about the fire and gives it real thought. He looks down at his own hands while he works through it.

"Wood was still on it," he says. "That's what I didn't like after. I'd put two splits on at the second hour. In the morning one was hardly black. The fire'd just gone small. Low and orange, not much heat off it. Like it was wet." He shrugs his heavy shoulders. "It wasn't wet. I cut it myself, dry deadfall."

Then he deals with the horse. "Stable yard behind this house hires out. Ask the boy." He jerks his chin toward the back door rather than pointing.

When Pell gets to his share, Tam takes his time over that too. "Anything on the road that's worth money, it's split even. Five ways." He glances at Ilse. She nods once. Then he hears Pell's rule. "You wave, I get down. I'm too big to ask why."

Orla comes past with a tray of empties and has clearly heard every word, the stable included.

"Two silver a day for a horse that won't throw you, and a deposit the boy will tell you," she says to Sefa without breaking stride. "Letter after you've eaten. I'm not having stew on it."

She keeps her word. When the bowls are scraped she comes back, wipes the end of the board with her apron and lays down a folded sheet. Its red wax seal was cut cleanly with a knife, not torn. The device is a plain ship's lamp in a ring. The hand is a clerk's, small and upright:

*To the keeper of the Burning Wizard, Beregost.*

*The house of Halvering asks whether its factor, Jory Fenwick, has come to your house or sent word through it. He left Nashkel near a month past with three wagons of the house's bar iron, bound for the Gate. A room was to be held for him with you. He also carries a small case under this house's seal, to be put into the hands of Brother Anselm at the Song of the Morning and no other. The temple writes that it has not been delivered.*

*The house will pay for reliable word of Master Fenwick, his wagons or the case, sent to the Halvering counting-room off the Wide, in the Lower City.*

*— for the house, M. Halvering*

Orla stands by with her arms folded until all three have read it, then holds out her hand for it.

"There. Now you know what I know." She tucks it back into her apron. "Brother Anselm's real enough. Tall, bald, up at the temple every morning before the bells. If you want him, he'll be there tomorrow."

The common room has got louder while they read. Someone at the carters' table is losing at knucklebones and saying so. Outside, the smithies have gone quiet at last.
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text

"Pairs. Good." She waits a moment. "I'll take the third hour."

Tam turns to Sefa's question about the fire and gives it real thought. He looks down at his own hands while he works through it.

"Wood was still on it," he says. "That's what I didn't like after. I'd put two splits on at the second hour. In the morning one was hardly black. The fire'd just gone small. Low and orange, not much heat off it. Like it was wet." He shrugs his heavy shoulders. "It wasn't wet. I cut it myself, dry deadfall."

Then he deals with the horse. "Stable yard behind this house hires out. Ask the boy." He jerks his chin toward the back door rather than pointing.

When Pell gets to his share, Tam takes his time over that too. "Anything on the road that's worth money, it's split even. Five ways." He glances at Ilse. She nods once. Then he hears Pell's rule. "You wave, I get down. I'm too big to ask why."

Orla comes past with a tray of empties and has clearly heard every word, the stable included.

"Two silver a day for a horse that won't throw you, and a deposit the boy will tell you," she says to Sefa without breaking stride. "Letter after you've eaten. I'm not having stew on it."

She keeps her word. When the bowls are scraped she comes back, wipes the end of the board with her apron and lays down a folded sheet. Its red wax seal was cut cleanly with a knife, not torn. The device is a plain ship's lamp in a ring. The hand is a clerk's, small and upright:

*To the keeper of the Burning Wizard, Beregost.*

*The house of Halvering asks whether its factor, Jory Fenwick, has come to your house or sent word through it. He left Nashkel near a month past with three wagons of the house's bar iron, bound for the Gate. A room was to be held for him with you. He also carries a small case under this house's seal, to be put into the hands of Brother Anselm at the Song of the Morning and no other. The temple writes that it has not been delivered.*

*The house will pay for reliable word of Master Fenwick, his wagons or the case, sent to the Halvering counting-room off the Wide, in the Lower City.*

*— for the house, M. Halvering*

Orla stands by with her arms folded until all three have read it, then holds out her hand for it.

"There. Now you know what I know." She tucks it back into her apron. "Brother Anselm's real enough. Tall, bald, up at the temple every morning before the bells. If you want him, he'll be there tomorrow."

The common room has got louder while they read. Someone at the carters' table is losing at knucklebones and saying so. Outside, the smithies have gone quiet at last.
````

"Maybe," he says. A long breath. "Still had it, Halloway. Whoever took it."

That's all he says about it. Ilse is the one who answers the part about the watch. She glances at Bram's spoon hand, then at his face, which is rare for her.

## Round 5

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 5 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram reads the letter twice. His lips move a little over the name. He hands it back to Orla without folding it, careful to keep his fingers off the cut seal.

"Thank you, mistress. That's more than most would show a stranger." He sets two coppers on the board beside his empty cup. It's not a payment so much as manners.

He waits until she's gone, then leans in toward the two tables.

"Three wagons of bar iron, near a month gone. Ambrel's tail wagon, eleven days. What was Ambrel's load, Brodder? If that was iron too, I'd call it a pattern." He taps the board once with a thick finger. "Nobody takes bar iron to sell in Nashkel. They've got mines for that. You'd move it north, or sell it quiet to someone who doesn't want to be seen buying it. And Beregost is a town full of forges."

He turns his cup a quarter turn, the way Tam did.

"The case is the odd part. You don't send a sealed case to a priest of Lathander by an iron factor unless you trust the factor more than the road. Or unless you don't want anybody guessing it's there." He looks at Sefa and at the man with the satchel. "Tomorrow we've a day while the horses rest. I mean to be at the Song of the Morning before the bells and ask Brother Anselm what was in it. Not demand it. Just ask, and watch his face when I do. A hospice hand might get further with a priest than an old guard would, if you'd walk up with me, Marrin."

Then, to Tam:

"And it might be worth an hour among the smiths. Has anyone been buying bar iron lately and paying too well for it? Smiths gossip about prices the way carters gossip about roads." His mouth twitches. "I grew up in a smithy yard. I know how to stand at a forge door and say nothing until they talk."

He sits back against the wall, his shield within reach, and finishes the last of his ale.

"That's tomorrow. Tonight I sleep behind a bolt." He looks at the man with the satchel again, steady and patient. "And you still haven't told us what to call you."

Sefa Marrin: “Thank you, Orla.”

I let her take the letter, then turn back to the table. “I’ll call on Brother Anselm tomorrow. He may know whether there was anything about that case that could put Fenwick in danger. And the temple ought to know we’re going to look.”

To Tam, I say, “I don’t recognise what happened to your fire. But dry wood left unburnt is worth remembering. If it happens again, we wake everyone—not just feed it.”

I ease myself up from the bench. “I’ll ask about the horse before I turn in. Would either of you like to come to the temple in the morning?” I look to Bram and Pell. “We could meet here over breakfast.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell reads the letter with his head tilted a little, twice—once for the hand, once for the words. When he's done, he sits back and lets Orla take it.

"He never came to your door," he says, not quite asking. "A factor with three wagons of bar iron and a room held for him doesn't ride past Beregost on his way to the Gate. Unless he meant to, and that's worth knowing too."

He taps two fingers on the table, a soft drum. "Small case under house seal, to one brother and no other. That's not a promise of payment. That's the kind of thing a house doesn't want carried by the ordinary post." A pause. "If I'm reading it right, the iron was the cargo and the case was the errand. One can be replaced. The other's why the house still writes."

He looks toward the temple road, though there's only wall and shutters to see. "I'll go up to the Song of the Morning at first bell and ask Brother Anselm what he knows. Not to pry into temple business—just whether he expected it, and whether anyone came asking after it." Then, to the table again: "If Fenwick was taken for the case, it won't look like wagon-thieving. It'll look like something done quiet. So when we stand at that waystone, I'll be looking for quiet, not for wheel ruts that stop."

He drains the last of his ale and sets the cup down. "I'll stable no horse. I walk faster than I look, and I'd rather climb the hill on my own feet." To Tam and Ilse: "Tell the boy I'll be at the yard before dawn on the day. I don't need a saddle."

Then he gives Orla a nod. "I'll settle the copper at breakfast. If I'm still breathing, I'll want the porridge hot and the ale later."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla takes the letter back flat, the way Bram handed it to her, and slides it into her apron. Her eyes go to the two coppers on the board. She sweeps them in with the side of her hand and gives one short nod. Then she's away to the carters' table, where the knucklebones loser wants another jug.

When Bram asks about the load, Tam takes his time.

"Iron," he says. "Ore, not bar. Sacks of it, straight from the Nashkel pits. The tail wagon was the heavy one. That's why it rode last." He lays his hands flat again. "Thing is, Halloway, it was coming here anyway. Ambrel sells his ore to Hask, at the long forge down by the Coast Way bridge. Anybody who wanted that ore could have bought it off Hask next week."

He stops for a moment, frowning at his own words.

"Two mules hauling a wagon of ore. Onto shale. And no sound." He shakes his head once. "That's a heavy thing to lift off."

Ilse watches Bram turn his cup, and her eyes stay on his hand a moment longer than they need to. "Hask was shorted," she says. "He'll talk about it."

Tam answers Sefa's point about the fire with a slow nod. "Wake everyone. Not feed it." He says it back to her as if he's fixing it in place. "Yes."

Pell talks about walking, and Tam looks at him under his heavy brows. "Waystone's a long day by wagon. Twenty-odd miles. Mounted, we're there by midday. On foot you get there by dark, and you'll have walked the lot." That's all he says. It's Pell's business how he gets there.

Orla comes back past with the full jug and has heard the talk of porridge and first bell. She doesn't stop walking.

"One silver, not a copper," she tells Pell. "Supper and the ale." Then, to the table at large: "The Song rings at sunrise. My kitchen's lit an hour before that. Porridge'll be hot if you want it before you go up."

Tam and Ilse stay where they are. Tam goes back to his cup and Ilse to not drinking hers. The man with the satchel still hasn't given a name, and nobody else at the table asks again.

---

The back door opens onto the stable yard. It's colder out here, and it smells of straw and horse, with the last of the forge smoke sitting low over the roofs. A lantern hangs from a hook by the stalls. Under it a boy of about twelve is forking straw. He has a mop of straw-coloured hair and a bigger man's jerkin belted twice round him. He stops when Sefa comes out and leans on the fork like a man twice his age.

"Horse, is it?" he says before she can ask. "Two silver a day. I've got a grey mare called Nettle, steady as a table, doesn't bite unless you're stupid round her mouth. Deposit's fifteen gold, and you get it back when she comes back sound." He jerks his head at the far stall, where a grey nose has come over the door to see who's talking. "No lame, no cuts, no sold-her-in-Nashkel. How many days d'you want her for?"

 (Out of game: none of these needed a roll. The turn showed the first three: Orla took the letter back flat and swept up the two coppers, and Tam said the load was ore. The temple visit is set for tomorrow before the bells, so it gets played when Bram goes up.)
````

## Round 6

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram sets the empty cup upside down on the board, an old camp habit meaning *done*, and lets the question of the man's name go. A man is entitled to keep his name in his pocket until he's ready to spend it.

"Ore, then. Not bar." He turns that over. "So it isn't the metal they want, or not only the metal. Either they wanted the wagon, or they wanted to see if they could do it." He looks at Tam. "Hask at the long forge. I'll go down after the temple, mid-morning, once the fires are up and he's in a talking mood. Come if you like. A man who was shorted talks easier to the guard who lost it than to a stranger." His tone is level. It's an offer, not a dig.

He pushes himself up from the bench and settles the shield on his arm out of habit.

"Day after tomorrow, first light, in the yard. I'll want a horse that can carry this much iron on its back without complaining." He taps his chain mail. "I'll go see the boy now, before the good ones are spoken for."

He nods to Ilse, then to Tam. "Third hour's yours, Var. I'll take the first."

---

Out in the yard Bram stops at a polite distance while the boy finishes with Sefa. He looks over the stalls with a caravan man's eye: hooves, legs, how the animals stand. When the boy is free, he steps up.

"Same terms for me, lad. But I'm heavier than the lady, with sixty pounds of mail on top. Have you anything with a broad back and short legs? Something that'd rather walk all day than run an hour." He pulls a silver bit from his pocket and holds it up between two fingers. "That's for you, not the house, if you put me on the right one and not just the one you want rid of. I'll take her for four days. Deposit I can pay at first light the day we ride."

He waits for the answer. Then, whatever it is, he goes back in, climbs the stair to the left-hand room, shoots the bolt and checks it twice. He props the shield where his hand will fall on it and lies down with his sword beside the bed.

Sefa Marrin: “Four days to start, leaving the morning after tomorrow. If we’re delayed, can I pay the extra days when we return?”

I approach the stall where Nettle can see me, keeping my hands away from her mouth.

“Steady is exactly what I need. I’m not an experienced rider. Does the hire include saddle and bridle?”

I look back at the boy. “And your name? I’d rather know who I’m trusting with my deposit.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell watches the room while the others talk—not with the flat stare of a man looking for trouble, but the slow, habitual pass of someone checking which floorboards creak and who flinches when the door bangs open. He eats without hurry, scraping the last of the barley into the spoon, and lets the conversation around him settle into a lower murmur.

When he's done, he wipes the bowl with the last torn bit of bread, stacks cup and bowl neatly at the edge of the table, and rises without pushing the bench back too far. A nod to Tam and Ilse, not unfriendly. "I'm still in," he says, low enough for just the table. "I'll be back before the kitchen fire's lit."

He carries the empty things to the counter, not calling to Orla, just setting them down near her elbow and waiting to catch her eye. When she turns, he says, "Porridge before the kitchen wakes. And the silver." He counts it out slowly: one for the cot, one for the supper and ale. "If the front's barred at midnight, I'll be in well before. If it isn't, I might take some air."

He doesn't ask about Fenwick outright; not yet. But he does ask, in the same neutral tone, "Anyone else come nosing about the Halvering name in the last fortnight? Or about a man with a small case and a temple errand?" A pause, just long enough. "I ask because if someone did, they'll have left by now, and if they didn't, the field's still fresh."

The room has grown warm, and the lantern flames bend a little when the front door opens for a pair of smiths shaking soot from their coats. Pell doesn't turn at the sound—just lets it register, and keeps his eyes on the innkeeper.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram turns the empty cup upside down on the board. Tam looks at it, and something in his broad face eases a little. It's a habit he knows.

"I'll come," he says about Hask. He takes a moment over it. "He'll want to look at me while he tells it. Better I'm there for that." He lays one hand flat on the board. "Mid-morning. I'll find you at the temple steps after the bells, Halloway."

When Bram gives her the third hour, Ilse nods once. Her eyes go to the upturned cup, then away.

---

Out in the yard the lantern has drawn moths. The boy has his fork planted in the straw and is in the middle of answering Sefa.

"Pay the extra days when she's back, yes. Two a day, counted off the deposit or out of your hand, your choice." He ticks it off on his fingers without letting go of the fork. "Saddle, bridle, blanket, all in the price. They come back with her, or they come off the deposit too." He watches how she stands by Nettle's door, hands well clear of the mare's mouth, and gives a short approving sniff. "Not a rider, you said. Good. Better you say it here than she finds out on the road. She'll follow the horse in front. Keep your heels quiet and your hands low and she'll do the rest." Then, as if he's been asked his price: "Dannet. Deposit when you lead her out, morning after tomorrow. I'll chalk your name on her door tonight so nobody else gets her."

Nettle pushes her grey nose over the half-door and sniffs Sefa's sleeve, and finds nothing worth biting.

Bram has been waiting at a polite distance, looking along the stalls. Reading horses is his trade, and he doesn't need long. There's a tall chestnut near the gate that holds its head up and looks fine, but it rests one forefoot like it's had trouble with it. There's a pair of carter's mules that belong to somebody else. In the end stall there's a short, deep-chested bay cob with feathered fetlocks and a back as broad as a bench. It stands square on all four feet and doesn't bother to look round.

Dannet sees him looking at the chestnut and jerks his head the other way.

"Not him. He looks a treat and he'll be lame by Lionshead." He walks to the end stall and slaps the cob's rump. The cob takes no notice. "Brick. Fourteen hands, carries a smith and his anvil, walks from dark to dark. Won't run if you beg him, so don't beg him." He looks at the silver between Bram's fingers, then up at Bram's face, every inch a dealer. "Four days, two a day, deposit fifteen gold at first light, and I'll chalk you on his door. And keep that bit till you've seen him walk out in the morning. I don't take money for a horse you've only seen standing."

The cob sighs through his nose and goes back to his hay. As far as Bram's eye can tell, he's sound: clean legs, a good hoof, and no heat anywhere.

Bram goes back in through the warm kitchen passage and up the stair to the left-hand door. The bolt runs true. He shoots it and tries it twice. The shield goes against the bedframe, where his hand will fall on it, and the sword lies along the blanket beside him. Below him the common room is a low murmur through the boards.

---

Orla turns from the tap and finds Pell's stacked bowl and cup at her elbow. That gets a look. Most of her customers leave their dishes where they ate.

Pell counts out his silver. One coin is all his purse holds. She lays a finger on it before he can make it two.

"Cot's paid. You gave me five at the table." She sweeps the single coin into her apron. "That's the supper and the ale. You're square." She's heard the rest of it. "Porridge it is, when the kitchen's lit. Not before. The kitchen wakes when I do." She tips her head at the front door, where two smiths are still beating soot off their coats. "Bar goes across at midnight. After that you knock, and loud, and I come down in a temper."

She gives his question some thought, wiping a ring off the counter as she does.

"Nobody's come asking about a case. Nobody knew there was a case except that house and the temple, from the sound of that letter. Now you three know." She puts the rag down. "About the factor, though, one did. A temple lad came down from the Song, ten days back or near it. Young, shaved head, out of breath from the hill. He asked had the Halvering factor come in yet. I told him no." She shrugs. "I'd guess that's how the temple knew to write."

One of the smiths calls for two ales. She's already reaching for the jug.

*(Out of game: Pell's purse held only one silver, and the cot was already paid, so that one coin settles his tab.)*

Upstairs, Bram is behind his bolt. In the yard Dannet is chalking two names on two stall doors. Nothing else happens tonight unless one of you wants it to.

## Round 7

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 7 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram lies awake a while in the dark and listens to the house: the murmur from below, a cart wheel out in the street, the boards settling. It's an old habit too. Once he knows how the place sounds when it's sleeping, he'll know when it isn't.

Before he settles, he gets up once and checks the shutter latch, careful not to lean on it, as Orla told him. He looks down at the yard. The lantern by the stalls is still burning, and there's nobody out there but moths. He goes back to bed.

Fire that burns low. Dogs that don't bark. A case for a priest. He turns it over twice and then lets it go. A man who chews on a thing all night has nothing left to chew with in the morning.

He sleeps the way a guard sleeps, lightly but properly.

---

He's up before the kitchen fire is lit. He hears it catch below, washes his face in the basin, and buckles on the mail without hurrying. Shield on his arm, sword at his hip. He leaves the javelins and the pack bolted in the room.

In the common room he takes a seat by the wall again and asks for porridge. If Sefa is down, he nods her over. If she isn't, he waits for her and doesn't make a show of it.

"Morning, Marrin. I'll do the asking up there if you like, but you watch him. I read men who are about to swing at me. A priest who's lying is a different animal." He eats steadily. "We say plainly who we are. Strangers who sat with the man who lost a wagon, and read the Halvering letter at the innkeeper's table. Nothing about the case unless he raises it, or until we have to. I want to see what he says before he knows how much we know."

When the bowl is empty, he leaves the copper on the board and heads out with Sefa into the cold grey street. They go up toward the Song of the Morning, so they're on the steps before the bells.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The Burning Wizard takes a long time to go quiet. For the first hour the common room comes up through the boards as a murmur: a laugh, a bench scraping, the knucklebones man losing again and saying so. Then the voices drop off one by one. Somebody comes up the stair heavily and goes along to the long room, and a door at the end shuts. Out in the street a cart rolls past slowly with one wheel ticking, a dry axle. Somewhere toward the Coast Way bridge a dog barks at something, gets an answer from another dog, and both give up. Under it all the house settles the way old timber does, a creak here and a tick there as the beams cool.

Bram gets up once in the dark and lifts the shutter latch without putting any weight on the frame. The yard below is as he left it. The lantern still hangs on its hook by the stalls with a slow cloud of moths around it. Chalk shows pale on two stall doors, and Brick's broad rump fills the end stall. Nobody's out there. He drops the latch and lies back down.

Around midnight the front bar goes across downstairs with a wooden thud, and Orla's tread goes up a back stair. After that the house belongs to the carters' snoring, which comes faintly through the wall from the long room, steady as a bellows.

He sleeps.

---

He wakes to the sound of the kitchen fire. It's still dark, but the dark has started to thin. Below him a grate is being riddled, kindling cracks as it catches, and a pot lid clangs. The basin water is cold enough to make him blink. He buckles the mail on piece by piece, settles the shield and belts on the sword. The pack and the javelins stay behind the bolt.

The common room is cold and grey. The tables have been wiped and the benches are still upside down on half of them. Only one lamp is lit. Orla is at the hearth in a shawl with her sleeves pushed up, feeding split wood under a black pot. A carter sits alone near the door with his elbows on the board, waiting for the same thing as Bram and saying nothing. Bram takes his seat by the wall.

Orla looks over her shoulder at him and at the mail. "Porridge it is. It's not thick yet. You'll have it thin or you'll wait." She waits about long enough to hear which. Then she ladles out a bowl, puts a heel of yesterday's bread beside it and sets both in front of him. "One copper."

The porridge is thin, hot and salted, and better than it looks. Through the front shutters the street is turning from black to slate. Up the hill, past the roofs, the Song of the Morning is still a dark shape against a lighter sky. The bells are maybe an hour off. Bram eats steadily, lays the copper on the board, and keeps one eye on the foot of the stair.

Nobody has come down it yet.

*(Out of game: Sefa and Pell, your turn. Do you come down to breakfast, and are you heading up to the temple? Bram's words to Sefa will wait until she's actually at his table.)*
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text

Sefa Marrin: “Sefa Marrin,” I give Dannet for the chalk. “Thank you. I’ll be here for her the morning after tomorrow.”

I head upstairs, check the room’s shutters without leaning on them, and bolt the door. I set my boots and pack within reach, say the hospice’s short evening prayer, and go to bed.

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Dannet already has the chalk in his hand. He wipes the half-finished letters off Nettle's door with the side of his fist and writes it out again, slowly, tongue between his teeth: SEFA MARRIN, in square capitals that slope downhill.

"There. Nobody touches her now." He steps back to look at his work. "Morning after tomorrow, deposit in your hand when she walks out. She'll be fed and brushed before you're down."

Nettle snorts at the chalk dust and pulls her head back into the dark of the stall.

---

Sefa's room at the top of the stair is narrow and smells of old smoke and lavender someone put in the bedding a long time ago. Her shutter looks over the street instead of the yard. When she lifts the latch, careful to keep her weight off the frame, she can see the roofs stepping up the hill and, above them, the dark shape of the Song of the Morning with one small light burning in it. She drops the latch. The bolt runs true. Her boots go by the bed and her pack beside them, and the hospice's short evening prayer is said quietly into the cold room. Through the wall Bram's floorboards creak once and then go still.

The carters snore. Around midnight the front bar thuds across downstairs. She sleeps.

---

The kitchen fire wakes her. It's still dark, with the first grey just starting at the edges of the shutter. The water in the basin is cold enough to sting.

The common room is as grey as the light outside. Half the benches are still upside down on the tables, the one lamp is lit, and the carter by the door is now scraping out a bowl. Bram sits with his back to the wall, mail on, shield against the bench, halfway through his porridge. He sees her on the stair and nods her over.

Orla doesn't ask. A bowl, a heel of bread and a spoon are put down in front of Sefa almost before she sits. "Porridge. Thicker than his, he was early. One copper."

The porridge is hot and salted, and it has thickened in the last quarter hour.

Strangers who sat with the man who lost a wagon, and read the Halvering letter at the innkeeper's table. Nothing about the case unless he raises it, or until we have to. I want to see what he says before he knows how much we know."

Through the front shutters the street has gone from black to slate. Up the hill the temple is still a shape against the sky, with that one light in it. The bells are perhaps three-quarters of an hour off. Nobody else has come down the stair from the long room.

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 7 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell absorbs the news about the temple lad with a small nod, tucking it away like a key he might need later. When Orla turns to the smiths, he steps back from the counter, easy and unhurried.

"Then he'll likely know more than he put in the letter," he says, half to himself. "Or less, and be worried about the wrong thing. Either way, I'll hear it at first bell."

He heads for the stairs, moving along the edge of the room where the shadows sit deepest. At the foot of the steps he pauses and looks back once, not at anyone in particular—just letting the room fix itself in his mind: the closeness, the smoke, the two smiths shaking out their sleeves, the sellswords still at their cups, the door that leads to the stable yard.

Then he goes up, quiet on the treads, to the long room and the third cot from the window. He sets the satchel under the pillow, the shortsword close at hand, boots laced loose, and trusts himself to wake when the kitchen stirs.

(Pell's player has left the table: Pell walks out of the scene and is not played again.)
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The night before, Pell steps back from the counter while Orla reaches for the smiths' jug. Nobody watches him go. He keeps to the edge of the room, where the lamplight doesn't reach, and stops once at the foot of the stair to look back over the common room: the two smiths still knocking soot from their sleeves, Tam and Ilse over their cups, the back door to the yard on its latch. Then he goes up without a sound on the treads.

The long room is dark and smells of wool and boots. The carters are already snoring behind the curtain. He finds the third cot from the window, pushes the satchel under the pillow, sets the shortsword where his hand will find it and loosens his bootlaces without taking the boots off. The sound of the carters closes over him.

---

Back in the grey morning, Orla comes past the wall table with the ladle still in her hand. She looks at the foot of the stair, then at Bram and Sefa.

"Your third won't be wanting porridge." She says it the way she quotes a price. "Cot by the window was empty when I came down to light the fire. The blanket was folded and the front bar was still across, so he went out by the yard. Dannet says he heard nobody." She shrugs. "He's square with me. Paid his cot and his supper last night."

She goes back to the pot. The carter by the door scrapes his bowl. Up the hill the one light still burns in the Song of the Morning, and the bells are perhaps three-quarters of an hour off. Bram's words to Sefa about the priest are still hanging over the table.

*(Out of game: Pell's player has left, so Pell slipped out of Beregost before dawn and leaves the story here.)*
````

## Round 8

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 8 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Morning, Halloway.” I set a copper beside my bowl and warm my hands against it.

“You can start the asking. I’ll listen.” I lower my voice to match his. “But I’d rather not make this a test he doesn’t know he’s taking. A priest may keep someone else’s confidence without meaning us harm.”

I take a spoonful, then add, “Ask about Fenwick first, certainly. If he asks what we know, we tell him. We need to know whether that case needs special handling if we find it.”

I eat while the porridge is hot, keeping an eye on the stairs for our third tablemate.

Cold air blows in, the door bangs shut, and the lamp flame bends and straightens. Nobody else has come down. The stair stays empty all the way up to the landing, and the long room behind it is quiet except for one carter still snoring through the curtain. Orla has said it once already: the cot by the window is empty, the blanket is folded, and he left by the yard.

Outside the shutters the street has gone from slate to a paler grey. A woman goes by with two buckets on a yoke. Up the hill the Song of the Morning is starting to show its shape against the sky: a long roof, a squat bell-tower, and the one small light still burning in it. The bells are perhaps half an hour off, and the climb takes a quarter of that.

Sefa's words sit low between her and Bram, under the clatter of the pot lid at the hearth.

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 8 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram finishes the porridge and wipes the bowl with the heel of bread, the way the satchel man did last night. He keeps an eye on the stair and doesn't make a show of it.

When Orla straightens from the hearth, he speaks quietly so it doesn't carry to the carter by the door.

"Mistress, a question while the pot thickens. Hask, at the long forge. Is he a man you'd trust with your change? And has anybody new been in town lately, buying iron and paying over the odds for it? Not smiths. Buyers." He turns the empty bowl a quarter turn. "Strangers with coin tend to drink somewhere. I'd guess they drank here at least once."

He lets her answer in her own time. Then he nods toward the ceiling, at the long room.

"Our friend with the satchel, the one on the third cot. Did he come back last night? He said he'd be down before your fire was lit."

He waits a while longer. If Sefa comes down the stair, he lifts two fingers to bring her over, slides the bread toward her and lets her eat. If the sky over the Song starts going from slate to pale and she still hasn't come, he stands, settles the shield and leaves word with Orla:

"If Marrin comes down, tell her I've gone up to the temple steps and I'll wait there till the bells. Brodder's meeting us there after."

Then he goes out into the cold street and walks up the hill without hurrying. He watches the doorways the way he'd watch a road.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla straightens from the hearth with the ladle in her fist. She hears him out without looking at the carter by the door, who has gone anyway and left his bowl.

"Hask pays his slate on the tenday, every tenday. I'd trust him with my change." She knocks the ladle once on the rim of the pot. "Not with anything you want kept quiet, mind. He talks, and he's louder when he's been shorted. He's been loud this tenday."

She takes longer over the buyers and stirs while she thinks.

"Smiths have been grumbling that bar iron's dear since spring. That's smiths, though. They'd grumble at free." Another stir. "One I'd call a buyer. Last month, near the start of it. Good coat, southern cut, Amnian I'd say. Two nights in the room you're in, Halloway. He stood the smiths' table a round both nights and asked who had bar iron to spare and how much. He paid me in Amnian coin and didn't haggle over the rate, which nobody does." She shrugs. "Iron goes south to Amn all the time. He might have been nothing. I didn't ask his business. Nobody's asked me about him since, and I've not seen him back."

When he nods at the ceiling she looks at him the way she'd look at a man asking for a second bowl he's already eaten.

"Gone. I said. The cot was empty when I lit the fire and the blanket was folded. He went out by the yard." She turns back to the pot. "He's square with me. That's all I know of him."

The empty bowl sits a quarter turned on the board. Sefa is already at the table across from Bram with her own bowl, and her words about the priest are still waiting for his answer. Through the shutters the street has paled to grey. The bells are perhaps twenty-five minutes off, and the climb takes about fifteen.

*(Out of game: Sefa's already at your table, so the leave-word-and-go part doesn't come up. Bram still hasn't answered her. Answer her, then tell me when you two set off.)*
````

## Round 9

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 9 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods to Orla. "Thank you, mistress. That's worth more than the copper." He adds another to the board anyway.

He turns to Sefa and keeps his voice low.

"Sorry, Marrin. I was half listening to the pot and half to her." He weighs what she said honestly. "You've sat with more priests than I have. If you think it goes better your way, then we do it your way. You lead and I stand at your shoulder looking like what I am. All I'd ask is that we don't hand him the whole story before he's told us his."

He leans in a little.

"One more thing for the pile. Last month an Amnian stood the smiths a round in this room two nights running and asked who had bar iron to spare. He paid in Amnian coin and didn't haggle the rate. Might be nothing. Iron goes south all the time." He shrugs. "But a man who doesn't haggle is a man spending somebody else's money. Ask Hask about him later."

He gets up, settles the shield and looks out through the shutters at the grey street.

"Bells in twenty-odd minutes, and it's a fifteen-minute climb. Finish your bowl, then we go. I'd rather be early and cold than walk in after he's gone in to his prayers."

When Sefa is ready, he holds the door for her and steps out first into the cold. They walk up the hill toward the Song of the Morning, and he keeps half a pace to her street side, watching the doorways as they pass.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla sweeps the second copper off the board with the first and gives Bram the same look she gave Pell over the stacked bowls.

"That's two you've left me for nothing. I'll stop telling you things if it keeps on." She goes back to the pot.

Sefa has her bowl, and the street outside has gone from slate to a pale, damp grey. Bram stands, settles the shield and holds the door. The cold comes in around his legs.

---

Beregost is only half awake. The Coast Way is wet with dew, and the ruts hold thin puddles that show the sky. Two doors down, a smith's apprentice of about fourteen is raking last night's clinker out of a forge mouth into a barrow. He yawns hard enough to crack his jaw and doesn't look up. A woman is taking down the shutters of a baker's shop, and the smell of the first loaves drifts out after her. A spotted dog trots past in the other direction, very busy, nose down. Nobody is in the doorways who shouldn't be there. Bram has walked a hundred towns at this hour, and this one is as ordinary as any of them.

The street turns uphill off the Way between two rows of plastered houses, and the cobbles start. At this hour it's a steady climb, not a hard one. Halfway up, the hammering starts behind them, one forge and then a second, like men clearing their throats. The roofs drop away below. Past them the Coast Way runs north and south in a grey line until the mist swallows it.

The Song of the Morning sits on the crown of the hill inside a low wall of honey-coloured stone. It has a long roof and a squat square bell-tower at the near end, and the whole front faces east. The gate stands open. In the yard beyond, the flagstones have been swept, and a row of clay pots along the wall holds marigolds still closed against the cold. The single lamp they saw from the inn burns in a niche over the main doors. Up close it's an ordinary brass lamp with its wick turned low.

There are two people in the yard.

A young acolyte with a shaved head stands at the foot of the bell-tower with the rope in both hands. He isn't pulling yet. He watches the eastern sky like a man waiting for a signal. He's thin, perhaps sixteen, and his robe is rose-coloured and too short at the wrists.

The other stands on the top step of the temple with his back to the doors, facing east where the sky over the hills is just beginning to turn from grey to the colour of watered wine. He is tall, taller than Bram, bald, and his scalp is pink with cold. His vestments are rose and old gold, with the hem darkened by dew. His hands are folded loosely in front of him. He has heard the gate and the sound of mail on cobbles, and he turns his head before they are halfway across the yard.

He takes them in one at a time: the shield, the mail and the sword, then the young woman beside them. His face doesn't change. He looks back at the sky, judges how long he has, and comes down two steps to meet them.

"You're early for the dawn and too late for the night lamp," he says. His voice is low and even, the voice of a man who reads aloud for a living. "The sun's a few minutes off. If you've come to greet it, stand on the east side of the yard and you're welcome. If you've come for me, say so now. Once the bell goes, the sick start coming to the gate, and they come first."

At the tower, the acolyte's eyes have moved from the sky to the two strangers. His hands stay on the rope.

 *(Out of game: none of the three needed a roll. Bram's words to Sefa and the Amnian lead are only talk, and the walk up the hill has already been told. My mistake was skipping the talk in the story, so here it is, at the table before you left.)*

Before Bram stood up, he spoke to Sefa quietly across the board, under the noise of the pot lid. He told her she could take the lead with the priest and he would stand at her shoulder. His one request was that they not give Anselm the whole story before Anselm had told them his. Then he told her about the Amnian: the rounds for the smiths, the coin, the rate he never haggled. He said it was one for Hask later. It was spoken low enough that Orla, at the hearth, gave no sign she'd heard. Then he got up, settled the shield, looked out at the grey street and went to the door. The walk up the hill and the scene in the temple yard went as told above.

Brother Anselm is still standing two steps down, waiting to hear whether they've come for the dawn or for him.
````

Back at the inn table, Sefa set her spoon in the empty bowl and said it: "Looks as though our quiet friend has his own morning in hand. Shall we?" Then she shouldered her pack and went out into the grey street with Bram half a pace to her street side.

Now she's in the swept yard of the Song of the Morning, and her pack straps are cold through her sleeves. The main doors behind Brother Anselm are shut. They're heavy oak, banded in brass, with the low-burning lamp in its niche above them. Nobody is inside to give room to, because nobody has been let in yet. The only worshippers here are the boy at the rope and the tall bald man two steps down, who came to meet them.

He hasn't moved since he spoke. His hands are still folded loosely in front of his vestments, and the dew-dark hem lies still on the stone. He looks at Sefa, then at Bram. Bram has taken his place at her shoulder and hasn't opened his mouth, so the priest turns back to her. He has noticed which one of them is going to talk.

Over the eastern hills the watered-wine colour has deepened toward rose. At the foot of the tower the acolyte shifts his grip on the rope, and the rope creaks. A few feet beyond the gate, out on the cobbles, an old man with a bandaged hand has stopped. He is leaning on the wall to wait. He's the first of the sick.

Brother Anselm's question still waits for an answer. Did they come for the dawn, or for him?

## Round 10

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “For you, Brother, if you have a little time before the bells. I’m Sefa Marrin; this is Bram Halloway.”

I keep my voice low in the quiet yard. “Orla at the Burning Wizard showed us the Halvering letter. We’re joining two caravan guards to look for the missing wagons, and hoped you might tell us about Jory Fenwick—whether you know him, and when you last had word.”

I glance toward the man waiting by the wall. “We can wait until after prayers if you’re needed.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Brother Anselm hears her out. When she says the Halvering letter, his folded hands tighten a little and then go loose again. His eyes go east. The rose over the hills has a hard bright edge on it now.

"I have a few minutes. Not more." He comes down the last step, so that he stands on the flagstones with them, and lowers his voice to match hers. "I know Master Fenwick. Not well. He's stopped here twice on the Way in other years. He leaves a coin at the altar and stays for the dawn if he's in town for it. He's a careful man and keeps careful hours." He pauses. "He wrote to me from Nashkel. The post rider brought it a little more than three weeks ago. He said he was leaving with the wagons the next morning and would be at my door inside the tenday. He's never missed a day he named. When the tenday was out I sent Teo down to the inn to ask. When the second tenday was out I wrote to his house."

He says nothing about the case. The space where it ought to come stays open while he looks at her, and then at Bram's shield, and back.

"You've read the letter, so you know what he was bringing me." He says it evenly, a statement and not a question. "If you find him, I want to know whether he's alive before anything else. If you find what he carried, I'd ask you to bring it here with the seal whole and not try what's under it." That's as far as he goes. He doesn't tell them what is under it.

At the tower the boy has gone up on his toes. The first sliver of sun breaks over the hills and lays a line of light across the top of the yard wall. Anselm sees it in the boy's face before he looks for it himself.

"Teo."

The rope comes down. The bell speaks once, deep enough to feel in the flagstones, then again, then again, and down the hill a dog answers it. Anselm turns full east and stands with his face in the new light for as long as the bell rings. His lips move, but no words reach them. When the last stroke has faded he turns back. Out on the cobbles the old man with the bandaged hand has pushed himself off the wall. Behind him a woman is coming up the street with a child on her hip.

"They come first," Anselm says. "Wait in the yard if you want me. When the gate is clear I'll come back to you, and we'll talk properly. It takes as long as it takes. On a quiet morning that's till the sun is a hand above the hills." He looks at Sefa a moment longer. "Or come back after noon, when it's quieter still."

He walks past them toward the gate, and his dew-dark hem drags over the stone. The old man holds up his bandaged hand, and Anselm takes it in both of his own.
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stops at the foot of the steps, a respectful distance off, and moves the shield behind his hip so it isn't the first thing the priest sees of him. He dips his head. It isn't a temple bow, but it's honest.

"For you, Brother, if you're Anselm. We'll be quick, and we'll stand aside when the bell goes. The sick should come first."

He gives his name plainly. "Bram Halloway. Caravan guard up from Nashkel, paid off and out of work." He nods at Sefa beside him. "This is Sefa Marrin, down from the Gate. She knows a hospice better than I know a temple, so she'll do most of the talking."

He steps half a pace back to Sefa's shoulder, where he said he would be, and lets her take it from there.

While she talks, he keeps quiet and turns a little so he faces east like the priest, out of courtesy to the sun. From that angle he can watch the priest's hands and face out of the corner of his eye, and the acolyte's too. He doesn't stare. He watches the way he'd watch a man at a toll gate: easy, and paying attention.

If the sun clears the hills while they're talking, Bram goes quiet and stands still for it the way everyone else in the yard does. He doesn't know the words, but he can at least keep his mouth shut.
