**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: I wrote last round that Bram hadn't opened his mouth. That was me getting ahead of you. He spoke first, as below, and then Sefa took it up.)*

At the foot of the steps, before Sefa had said a word, Bram stopped a respectful distance off and moved the shield back behind his hip. He dipped his head. It wasn't a temple bow, but the priest's eyes followed it all the way down and back up.

"For you, Brother, if you're Anselm. We'll be quick, and we'll stand aside when the bell goes. The sick should come first."

Something in the long face settled when he said that. It wasn't warmth. It was more like a man ticking off a line he had expected to argue over. "I am Anselm. And they do."

"Bram Halloway. Caravan guard up from Nashkel, paid off and out of work. This is Sefa Marrin, down from the Gate. She knows a hospice better than I know a temple, so she'll do most of the talking."

He stepped half a pace back to her shoulder, and Sefa went on from there, low and plain. She mentioned the letter, the caravan guards and Fenwick.

Bram stood turned a little east, the way the priest stood, and watched from the corner of his eye. Anselm kept his hands folded the whole time Sefa spoke, except once. When she said *Halvering letter*, the fingers closed on each other and then opened again, the way a man's hand does when a weight shifts in a pack he's carrying. After that they stayed loose. They stayed loose through everything he said about Fenwick, and through the long gap where he didn't speak of the case.

Over at the tower, the boy didn't look at the strangers much. He looked at the sky. When Anselm said *I sent Teo down to the inn*, the acolyte's eyes flicked to the priest and then away, like any boy hearing his own name. Then the sun broke the hills and he forgot all of them.

When the bell spoke, Bram went still. He didn't know the words, so he kept his mouth shut and his eyes on the light coming up over the yard wall. The bell rang through the flagstones into his boots. When the last stroke died away, Anselm went past him toward the gate.

---

Now the yard has the new sun across the top of it, and the marigolds along the wall are starting to open. At the gate Anselm has the old man's bandaged hand between his own two. He is unwinding the cloth slowly and talking too quietly to carry. The old man winces once and then laughs at something. Behind him the woman with the child on her hip has reached the wall. The child is red-cheeked, with a wet cough that comes across the yard clearly enough. Teo has left the rope and gone in through a side door. He comes back with a basin, a cloth over his arm and a clay jar.

Out on the cobbles a broad shape is coming up the hill with the sun on one side of its face: Tam Brodder, sunburnt neck and all. He gets to the gate, sees who's being tended in it and stops. He stands aside against the outer wall to let the woman and the child go in first. Then he finds Bram across the yard and lifts his chin an inch.

"Halloway."

That's all. He stays where he is, outside the wall, and waits.

Anselm doesn't look up from the old man's hand. The sun is only a finger's width above the hills. He said he'd be free when it's a hand.
````

## Round 11

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't interrupt the work at the gate. He crosses the yard along the wall, away from the old man and the woman with the child, and stops beside Tam outside the gate. He keeps his voice low enough that it stays between the two of them.

"Brodder. Morning." He looks back toward the priest rather than at Tam. "He's Anselm, all right. Says he'll be free when the sun's a hand up. When Marrin named the Halvering letter, his hands closed on each other and then let go. He talked about Fenwick readily enough. He never said a word about the case." He shrugs slightly. "That might be a man keeping his house's business to himself. Or it might be a man carrying something heavy. We'll see."

Then he lifts his chin toward Teo and the basin.

"Lad's got his hands full." He steps to the gate and speaks over the woman's shoulder to the acolyte, plain and quiet. "Teo, is it? If there's water wants hauling or a man wants holding still, I've got two hands doing nothing. Tell me where the well is." If the boy points, Bram goes and fills whatever he's given. He sets it down where Teo wants it and steps back out of the way. He doesn't crowd the sick, and he isn't trying to earn anything by it. Standing idle in a yard where people are working sits wrong with him.

When the sun is a hand over the hills and Anselm straightens from the last of them, Bram comes back to Sefa's shoulder and lets her carry on. He leans close for a moment before she does.

"He went quiet on the case. If you're going to ask him about it, now's the time, while his hands are wet and he's still thinking about the sick." He steps back to his place. If Sefa leaves it alone, Bram waits for a pause and asks it himself, mildly and without pressing:

"Brother, the letter spoke of a case under the Halvering seal, meant for your hands and no one else's. We're riding down to the old waystone tomorrow, where Brodder's wagon went. If we find it out there, I'd sooner know what I'm holding than guess. Is it something a man should be careful carrying?"

He watches the priest's hands again while he answers.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram goes along the inside of the wall, keeping well clear of the gate where the old man's hand is being unwound, and steps out onto the cobbles beside Tam. The big man shifts over to give him room against the stone. Bram talks low, with his eyes on the priest, and tells it all: the hands closing at the words *Halvering letter*, the easy talk about Fenwick, and the case that never came up.

Tam takes his time, as he always does.

"Heavy, then," he says at last. He folds his arms over his chest. "Or careful. A priest can be both." He nods toward the yard. "I'll stop out here. Temples and me don't sit right. Hask's fires will be up by the time he's done anyway."

When Bram speaks over the woman's shoulder, Teo looks up. He has the basin balanced on one hip and the cloth slipping off his arm, and for a moment he looks at the mail and the sword as if he isn't sure the offer is real.

"Yes, sir. Teo." He jerks his head toward the bell-tower, then remembers his manners and points properly. "The well's round the back of the tower. There's a pail on the hook. The windlass sticks halfway up, you have to lean on it." He holds out the clay jar with both hands. "This one, please, and the pail as well, if you can. We go through it."

The well is where he said, a stone ring behind the tower with moss in the cracks and a wooden cover pushed half aside. The windlass sticks halfway up, just as the boy said. Bram leans on it and it gives with a groan. The water is black and cold enough to make his fingers ache. He fills the jar, then the pail, and carries them back around the tower. He sets them down on the flagstones by the gate where Teo points, a step behind the priest's left hand, and moves off out of the way.

Anselm doesn't look round, but his voice comes back over his shoulder, even and low. "Thank you." Then he goes back to work.

The work at the gate is ordinary. The old man's hand has a burn across the palm, an old one that has gone angry at the edges. Anselm washes it from the new pail, lays his own hand over it and says a few words under his breath. A faint warmth, like early sun on a wall, shows for a moment between his fingers and the old man's. The angry red softens to pink. The old man flexes the hand and laughs again, louder this time, and tries to press a copper on Teo. Teo won't take it and points him at the box by the doors.

The child is next. Anselm hasn't used his gift on her. He kneels on the wet stone in his vestments, puts his ear to her back and listens while she coughs. Then he talks to the mother for a while in a voice too low to carry. Teo goes inside and comes back with a small stoppered pot. The mother nods at everything, hitches the child up on her hip and goes off down the hill with the pot held tight in her fist.

Two more come after her: a smith's apprentice with a blistered forearm wrapped in a wet rag, and an old woman who mostly wants to sit on the wall and be asked how she is. Anselm asks her. He stays with her longer than he stays with the burn.

The sun climbs. The marigolds along the wall open the rest of the way, and the shadow of the bell-tower pulls in across the yard. By the time the last of them has gone off down the cobbles, the sun stands a good hand above the eastern hills, and the street outside the gate is empty except for Tam, still leaning on the wall with his arms folded.

Anselm straightens, dries his hands on the cloth Teo holds out, and stands for a moment rolling his shoulders like a man who has been kneeling too long. Teo picks up the half-empty pail and goes off toward the side door with it.

Bram comes back across the yard to Sefa's shoulder, leans in and says it quietly: if she means to ask about the case, now is the time, while the priest's hands are still wet and his mind is still on the sick. Then he steps back into his place.

Anselm walks back to them. The dew-dark hem of his vestments is darker still at the knees. He glances east once, and this time it isn't to judge the hour. It is more like a man checking he has earned the rest of the morning.

"The gate's clear," he says. "I'm yours until someone else comes up that hill. Ask what you came to ask."

*(Out of game: Sefa, he's looking at you. If she leaves the case alone, Bram's question comes next.)*

 *(Out of game: Bram's walk to Tam and the water from the well were both told in the story; neither was uncertain, so no roll was needed.)*

*(Out of game: Bram's question about the case only comes if Sefa leaves it alone, and her player hasn't chosen yet, so the turn stops with Anselm's "Ask what you came to ask." It isn't a roll either way. Asking a priest plainly is just talk.)*
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: I bow my head while the bell rings.

“We’ll put Fenwick first,” I tell Anselm before he goes to the gate. “We’ll speak after noon.”

I move aside so the woman and child have a clear path, then turn to Bram. “That leaves time for Hask’s forge. I’d still like to ask Anselm whether the case poses any danger to whoever carries it, but not while people are waiting for care.”

I settle my pack straps. “Shall we find Tam and Ilse?”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Back when the bell spoke, Sefa bowed her head and kept it bowed until the last stroke had gone into the flagstones. When Anselm turned toward the gate, she told him quietly, "We'll put Fenwick first. We'll speak after noon." He stopped for a moment, nodded once, and went on to the old man's hand without another word.

She stepped back off the path so the woman with the coughing child had a clear way in. Then she turned to Bram and kept her voice low. "That leaves time for Hask's forge. I'd still like to ask Anselm whether the case is any danger to whoever carries it, but not while people are waiting for care."

---

Now the gate is clear, and Anselm is standing in front of them with damp hands. He has just said *ask what you came to ask*, and he waits about a breath to see whether anyone will.

"You said after noon." He doesn't sound disappointed. He's simply putting it back on the table. "I hadn't forgotten. I offered now because now was free, and I know you've stood in my yard an hour for it." He dries the last of the water off his knuckles with the cloth Teo left. "After noon, then. Come to the side door under the bell-tower and ring there. Teo will find me wherever I am. Then we'll have it all, and nobody coming up the hill to cut it short."

He looks at Sefa a moment longer. Behind his eyes he's settling something.

"When you come, I'll have Master Fenwick's letter from Nashkel out on the table. You can read for yourselves what he wrote and when he wrote it. If you're going down that road, you should know the day he meant to be on it." Then he turns to Bram and nods toward the well. "And thank you for the water. Teo would have carried it in four trips."

He goes up the steps. One of the heavy oak doors opens under his hand onto a dim nave that smells of beeswax, and closes behind him.

Out past the gate, Tam pushes himself off the wall as Sefa settles her pack straps. He has heard none of it, but he can see it's finished. "Done?" he asks. He tips his head down the hill, toward the bridge and the forge smoke now rising thick and grey over the roofs. "Hask's lit. You can hear him from here." You can. Under the other hammers there's one slow, heavy beat, and a voice shouting at someone in between strokes. "Var's at the stable. Seeing to the horses. She'll come if she's sent for."

Sefa's question to Bram is still hanging in the cold yard: shall they find Tam and Ilse?

*(Out of game: Sefa put off the case until after noon, so Bram's fallback question hasn't been asked. Bram, do you ask it now, or keep to after noon?)*
````

````text
---
````

## Round 12

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “For now,” I tell Tam. “We’re coming back after noon to read Fenwick’s letter. Anselm wants him found alive before anything else.”

I fall into step beside him on the way down toward the forge.

“You said Hask was expecting the ore. Did he choose that particular load, or was Ambrel simply bringing what the pits had to sell?”

At the forge, I stay clear of the work and wait for Hask to acknowledge us rather than calling over a hammer stroke.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam hears her out by the gate. He nods once at "alive before anything else," the way he'd nod at an order he agreed with.

"After noon." He turns and looks back over the wall into the yard, where Bram is still standing on the flagstones by the steps. "Halloway. Forge." He doesn't wait for an answer. He starts down the cobbles beside Sefa, but slowly, slow enough that a man in mail could catch up with them before the bridge.

The hill is busier going down than it was coming up. A baker's girl climbs past them with a covered tray. Two carters argue over a wheel outside a wheelwright's. The forge smoke has settled thick and grey over the lower roofs. As they drop toward the Coast Way, that one heavy hammer gets louder, and so does the voice shouting between the strokes.

Tam gives her question the same slow attention he gives everything.

"Didn't choose it." He walks a few steps before going on. "Hask has a standing order with Ambrel. So many sacks every lot, same weight every time. Ambrel buys what the pits sell that week and carries it. Hask's paid for it before it leaves Nashkel." He jerks a thumb back up the hill, toward the road south and nothing in particular. "Same as the lot before. Same as the one before that. There was nothing special in that wagon, Marrin. Only that it was the heaviest, so it rode last."

They come down onto the Coast Way by the bridge. Hask's long forge stands on the river side of the road. It's a low building of black timber and stone, with the whole river end open to the air and three hearths in a row under one roof. Two are cold this morning. The third roars. A boy works the great bellows with both arms, and Sefa recognises the wet rag still wrapped round his forearm. He's the apprentice from the temple gate. He sees her, looks surprised, and nearly loses the rhythm.

"*Pull,* you—" The voice comes from the anvil, and the boy pulls.

Hask is easy to pick out. He is a short, enormously broad man in a scorched leather apron, mostly bald, with eyebrows singed down to stubble. His forearms are as thick as Sefa's calves. He has a bar of orange iron in the tongs and is drawing it out with a hand hammer in a steady four-beat. Every fourth beat he says something to the boy, to the iron, or to the world in general. None of it is kind.

Sefa stops well clear of the work, outside the spark line on the packed earth, and waits. Tam stops beside her and folds his arms.

Hask doesn't look up. He finishes the draw, turns the bar and gives it four more. Then he lifts it, sights along it and drops it hissing into the slack tub. Steam goes up around him in a white cloud. When it clears he's looking straight at Tam.

"Brodder." It carries across the forge as easily as the hammer did. "Come to tell me where my eleven sacks went? Or come to tell me again you don't know?" His eyes go to Sefa, quick, taking in the pack and the road dust. He doesn't change his voice for her. "And who's this? Ambrel's sending women now to say sorry for him?"

*(Out of game: Bram, Tam called you from the gate and walked slowly. Does Bram follow them down? If he does, he reaches the forge in time for this.)*
````


### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stays at Sefa's shoulder and says nothing at first. He gives her a short glance that means *your ground, go on*. The priest has earned a straight question, and Bram would rather it came from her.

While he waits, he looks at the priest's knees, dark with wet stone, and then at his hands. Those hands were steady on the old man's burn, and they closed on each other at the Halvering name.

If Sefa takes up the case, Bram keeps quiet and watches.

If she asks about something else and leaves the case alone, Bram waits for a pause and asks it himself. He keeps his voice low and leaves it room to be answered or not:

"Brother, one more thing, and you can tell me it's not mine to ask. The letter spoke of a case under the Halvering seal, meant for your hands and no one else's. We ride for the old waystone tomorrow, where Brodder's wagon went. If we turn that case up in a ditch out there, I'd sooner know what I'm holding than guess." He looks the priest in the face. "Is it a thing a man should be careful carrying? And if we find it, do I bring it to you sealed, or does the house want it first?"

Then he stops talking, keeps his hands still on the shield rim, and watches Anselm's hands for the answer before he listens to the words.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: I had Anselm go inside before Bram could answer, so I've moved that back. Bram catches him on the top step with his hand on the door.)*

Sefa puts off the case until after noon. Anselm nods, climbs the steps and puts one hand to the heavy oak door. Bram gives Sefa a short glance: *your ground*. She has already turned for the gate, and out on the cobbles Tam has called "Halloway. Forge." and started down the hill beside her, slowly. Only Bram is left in the yard to speak.

"Brother. One more thing, and you can tell me it's not mine to ask."

Anselm stops with the door half open. Beeswax-smelling dark breathes out past him. He doesn't turn all the way round, only far enough to look down at Bram over one shoulder.

Bram keeps his voice low. "The letter spoke of a case under the Halvering seal, meant for your hands and no one else's. We ride for the old waystone tomorrow, where Brodder's wagon went. If we turn that case up in a ditch out there, I'd sooner know what I'm holding than guess." He looks the priest in the face. "Is it a thing a man should be careful carrying? And if we find it, do I bring it to you sealed, or does the house want it first?"

Then he stops talking and keeps his hands still on the shield rim. He watches the priest's hands.

Anselm lets go of the door, and it swings shut on its own weight. His hands come together in front of his vestments. They don't tighten the way they did in the yard. They fold and rest, like a man putting down something he expected to carry further. His knees are dark with the wet stone. He looks at Bram for a long moment, and doesn't look at the sky.

"To me," he says. "Sealed. The house wrote *no other*, and the house meant it. They sent it away from themselves on purpose. Don't let anyone tell you it should go back to the Gate first."

He comes down one step, so he isn't talking down at Bram.

"Careful, yes. Not because it's heavy, or because it will bite you through the seal. As I understand it, closed, it will do you no harm." He stops there, and something behind his eyes weighs how much to say on a doorstep. "Don't open it. Don't let anyone you're riding with open it. If you find it and the seal is already broken, leave it in its case, wrap it in whatever you have, and bring it to me without looking."

That's more than he said in the yard, and Bram can see he knows it. His hands stay quiet through all of it. They don't move when he says *careful*, or when he says *don't open it*. A man who was lying would have done something with his hands about then.

"I'll tell you what I was told of it after noon, with Master Fenwick's letter on the table and the door shut," Anselm says. "Not on the steps." He looks at Bram's shield, at the painted-over mark, then back at his face. "You asked the right question, Master Halloway. Most men would have asked what it's worth."

He goes back up, opens the door and goes in. It closes behind him with a soft weight of oak and brass.

The yard is empty now apart from the marigolds and the half-empty pail by the gate. Down the hill, under the other hammers, Hask's heavy beat keeps time over the roofs. Sefa and Tam are a street or more ahead of Bram by now, and Tam is walking slowly enough to be caught.
````

## Round 13

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stands a moment longer at the foot of the steps, looking at the closed oak. Then he dips his head to the door, the same honest not-quite-bow as before. He picks up the half-empty pail by the gate and sets it inside the side door where Teo will find it. Then he goes out.

He lengthens his stride down the cobbles and catches up with Tam and Sefa before the street turns onto the Way. He falls in on the street side and keeps his voice low.

"Caught him at the door. Here's what he said, near enough word for word." He counts it off on his fingers. "If we find it, it goes to him, sealed. Not back to the Gate first, and the house meant *no other*. Closed, it'll do us no harm. Don't open it, and don't let anybody with us open it. If the seal's already broken, we wrap it in whatever we've got and bring it to him without looking." He lets that sit for a step or two. "His hands never moved, Marrin. Not once. I don't think he was lying. I think he's scared of it, though, and that's a different thing."

He glances at Tam. "So if your wagon-lifters took it, they may have opened it. Or they knew better than to. I'm not sure which I'd like less."

"After noon he'll tell us what he was told of it, door shut, Fenwick's letter on the table. He's got a letter from Fenwick, it seems. So the man wrote ahead at least once." He looks over at Sefa. "You set that hour, so I'll come with you, if you'll have me."

Then the heat and the hammering of the long forge are close, and Bram nods Tam ahead.

"Your ground now, Brodder. He's your smith and it was your wagon. I'll stand back and look like I'm admiring his tongs." His mouth twitches. "When he's done grumbling about the ore, I'll ask about the Amnian with the good coat."

He stops a polite pace inside the forge door, out of the way of the swing of the hammer, and does what his father taught him in the smithy yard: says nothing, watches the work, and waits for the smith to talk.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram stays at the foot of the steps a moment longer, looking at the shut oak, then dips his head to it the way he did before. The half-empty pail is still by the gate. He carries it across the yard and sets it inside the side door under the bell-tower, where Teo will trip over it soon enough, and goes out through the gate.

He lengthens his stride down the cobbles. Tam has kept his word about walking slowly, and Bram catches the two of them a street short of the Way. He falls in on the street side and gives them the doorstep word for word, counting each part off on his fingers: sealed, to Anselm, not back to the Gate; closed it does no harm; nobody opens it; if the seal is broken, wrap it and bring it without looking. He tells them the priest's hands never moved, and that he thinks Anselm is frightened of the thing rather than lying about it.

Tam walks a dozen steps on that before he answers.

"Fenwick went a month back. Mine went eleven days back." He watches the cobbles in front of his boots. "If the same men took both, they've had that case three weeks. Long enough to open it." He leaves it there.

Bram's question to Sefa, about coming with her after noon, gets no answer from Tam. It isn't his hour to give.

The heat of the long forge reaches them before the noise does, a warm push of air across the Way by the bridge. Bram nods Tam ahead and makes his remark about admiring the tongs. Tam doesn't smile, but one corner of his broad face shifts. Then the three of them come in under the black timbers. Hask quenches his bar in a cloud of steam and starts on Tam.

*Come to tell me where my eleven sacks went? Or come to tell me again you don't know?* Then his eyes go to Sefa. *Ambrel's sending women now to say sorry for him?*

Bram stops a pace inside the open end, well clear of the hammer's swing, and says nothing.

---

Tam takes the first question the way he takes everything, slowly, with his arms folded and his feet planted on the packed earth outside the spark line.

"Don't know." He lets that stand by itself. "Going back tomorrow. Me and Var. Find out."

"Going *back*." Hask pulls a fresh bar from the fire with the tongs, orange shading to yellow at the tip, and lays it on the anvil. The hand hammer comes down four times while he talks. "Ambrel paid you off." *Clang.* "Ambrel says paid in Nashkel means mine in Nashkel, and the road's my lookout." *Clang.* "So I'm out eleven sacks, he's out nothing, and you're out on the road again for free." *Clang, clang.* He turns the bar. "You were always a slow one, Brodder. Didn't know you were stupid as well."

Tam's arms stay folded. "Your sacks are on that road somewhere. So's my driver."

That gets a grunt. It isn't agreement, but it isn't another insult either. Hask lifts the bar, sights along it with one eye shut and puts it back in the fire. "*Harder*, boy, it's going grey on me." The apprentice leans into the bellows. The wet rag on his forearm flaps with every pull, and he keeps sneaking looks at Sefa.

Then Hask's eyes find Bram by the door: the mail, the shield, the sword, the way he stands still and out of the way like a man who has stood in a forge before. He looks Bram up and down once, bluntly, the way he'd look at a horse somebody else was trying to sell him.

"You. Stand there all you like. Don't touch my tongs." Then he turns back to Sefa with the bar heating behind him, and his stubbled brows go up. He's still waiting on her answer. Who is she, and is she here to say sorry for Ambrel?

*(Out of game: Hask's opening lines were spoken just as Bram reached the door. His question to Sefa is still hers to answer, and so is Bram's offer to come with her after noon.)*
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Sefa Marrin. Ambrel hasn’t sent me, and I don’t speak for him.”

I keep my voice level. “I’m going south with Tam to look for Corrin Dace and the wagon. He tells me your order was eleven sacks, already paid for. I’d like to know how we’d recognise them if we find them—marks on the sacks, or anything about the ore.”

I glance at the cold hearths, then back to Hask. “And have any other deliveries failed to reach you?”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hask hears her out and doesn't interrupt. That seems to cost him something. When she says *I don't speak for him*, his singed brows come down a little. He pulls the bar from the fire, looks at the colour and puts it back.

"Huh. Somebody who doesn't." He jerks his chin at Tam. "Then you're better company than he keeps."

He takes the question about the sacks seriously, which nobody would have guessed from his opening. He sets the tongs down across the anvil to answer it.

"Tarred hemp. Ambrel's wheel burnt into the side, a cart wheel with eight spokes, a hand across. Chalk tally on the neck from the pit." He holds up three thick fingers. "Number Three. That's the only pit he buys from. Eleven sacks, two hundredweight each, near enough. You won't miss the ore either. It's red kidney ore, lumps like a butcher's offal, and it stains your hands rust to the wrist. Rain makes it bleed. If there's a red puddle in a ditch somewhere, that's mine."

He looks at the two cold hearths when she does. He snorts.

"Those? Cold because I've nothing to feed 'em. Bar's been dear since spring and I won't pay what they're asking. My ore was meant to be those two." He picks the tongs up again. "Other deliveries? No. Mine came every lot, on the day, for six years. That tail wagon's the only one I've lost." The bar comes out bright orange and goes onto the anvil, and the hammer starts its four-beat while he talks. "The lot before Brodder's, the one that came in two wagons short. That wasn't mine." *Clang.* "That was bar iron for Taerom up the hill at Thunderhammer." *Clang.* "He lost a sight more than I did." *Clang, clang.* "Go and ask him how he likes it. He won't talk as nice as me."

He turns the bar, gives it four more and looks at Sefa over it. He's still rude, but it's a different kind of rude now.

"You find my sacks, you send word or you bring 'em. I'll pay a silver a sack for the carting, and that's more than Ambrel ever paid anybody to look." He sights along the bar with one eye shut. "Brodder. You hear that? She asked about my *ore*. Six days you've been back and you never once asked what it looked like."

Tam's arms stay folded. "Didn't need to. I loaded it."

Hask grunts, and it could almost be a laugh. The bar goes into the slack tub and the steam goes up white between them. The apprentice keeps the bellows going, and the wet rag on his arm flaps with every pull.

When the steam clears, the grumbling is over. Hask wipes his hands on the apron and glances once at the man in mail standing by the open end, as if he's wondering whether that one is going to say anything or just stand there.

*(Out of game: Bram, Hask is done with the ore. If you want the Amnian question now, it's yours.)*
````

## Round 14

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Eight-spoked wheel, Number Three, red kidney ore. Thank you. If we find it, we’ll send word. I won’t promise to haul eleven sacks without a wagon.”

I shift aside to leave Bram a clear place to speak, then ask, “Has anyone offered you unusually cheap iron since those wagons disappeared? Here or through a travelling seller?”

While Hask answers, I look toward the apprentice. “And lad—has that dressing been seen to this morning?”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hask snorts at the part about the wagon.

"Didn't ask you to haul 'em. Send word. I've a cart and a mule that hates me, and I'll fetch my own." He picks up the tongs, then sets them down again when she asks about cheap iron. He looks along his forge at the two cold hearths. They have black, dead ash in them, and a broom is leaning against the nearer one.

"Cheap?" His stubbled brows go up. "If somebody'd come in here offering me cheap iron, girl, would those be sitting cold?" He jerks the tongs at them. "Nobody's offered me cheap anything since spring. The pedlars that come through with a few bars on a mule want more than the factors, and they look at you like you should thank them." He takes a fresh bar out of the fire and lays it on the anvil. "If anybody turns up selling iron cheap on this road now, I'll ask him where he got it. I'll ask him with this." He lifts the hand hammer an inch, then brings it down on the bar instead.

The beat starts up again. The boy at the bellows doesn't answer at first, because he's counting the pulls under his breath. When Sefa's question gets to him, he goes red to the ears and keeps pulling.

"Yes, mistress. The brother did it at the gate this morning." He lifts his arm a little, careful to keep the rhythm. The rag is clean, and under it there's a shine of salve. "He said keep it wet and keep it out of the sparks, and come back tomorrow if it goes yellow." He glances at Hask, then back at Sefa. "It hasn't gone yellow."

"It'll go yellow if you stand there flapping it about," Hask says, without looking up. "Pull."

The boy pulls.

Four beats. Hask turns the bar, sights along it and puts it back in the fire. Then he wipes his forehead with his wrist and looks past Sefa, who has stepped aside, to the man in mail by the open end. He doesn't say anything this time. He just raises his singed brows, as if to say *well?*

Tam stays where he is with his arms folded and waits too.

*(Out of game: Bram, Sefa has left you a clear place to speak, and Hask is looking at you.)*
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram gives Hask one short nod at *don't touch my tongs*, the nod of a man who has heard the same thing from his own father a hundred times. He keeps his hands on the shield rim and stays put.

He lets Sefa answer for herself. He doesn't look at her while she does it. He watches the bar in the fire instead, the way his father taught him: if you watch the iron, the smith forgets you're there.

He notices the apprentice's wrapped forearm on the bellows. It's the same lad who was at the temple gate this morning, and Bram files that away.

When Hask has said his piece to Sefa and gone back to the anvil, Bram waits for the bar to come out of the fire and the first blows to land. Then he speaks into the gap between strokes. He keeps it level and offhand, as if they were talking about the weather.

"Bar iron's been dear since spring, I hear." *Clang.* "There was an Amnian through here last month. Good coat, southern cut. Stood the smiths' table a round at the Burning Wizard two nights running and asked who had bar to spare." He waits for the next stroke. "Paid in Amnian coin and never haggled the rate. Did he come asking at your door, master smith? And if he did, did anybody sell to him?"

Then he goes quiet again and watches the iron. If Hask bristles, Bram doesn't push. He just adds, mildly:

"I ask because three wagons of Halvering bar went missing on that road a month back, and your ore went eleven days ago. A man who doesn't haggle is spending somebody else's money. I'd like to know whose."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hask gives no sign he saw Bram's nod. He has gone back to the iron, and the iron is all he looks at. The bar comes out of the fire yellow at the tip. He lays it on the anvil and the four-beat starts.

Bram talks into the gaps. He mentions bar iron being dear since spring, then the Amnian in the good southern coat who stood the smiths' table at the Burning Wizard two nights running. Hask's hammer doesn't miss a stroke. When Bram gets to *never haggled the rate*, one stubbled brow goes up, and it stays up.

"Him." *Clang.* "I drank his ale." *Clang.* "Both nights. Free is free." *Clang, clang.* He turns the bar. "Asked me did I have bar to spare. I laughed in his face. Look at my hearths." He jerks his chin at the two cold ones without looking round. "He laughed too. Polite. He knew already. He'd asked the room before he asked me."

The bar goes back in the fire. "Harder, boy." The apprentice leans into the bellows, and Hask wipes his forehead with his wrist and goes on in his own time.

"Here's what I didn't like, since you're asking. He didn't want what I had. Nobody's got any, and he knew that. What he wanted was who brings it up. Which factors ride the Way, what days they leave Nashkel, how many wagons. Said he'd sooner buy off the wagon at the road than pay a smith's price after." He snorts. "Fair enough. That's trade. Any buyer who wants it cheap goes to the factor. Only he asked it the way you'd ask about weather, and he bought the next round before anybody had finished answering."

Bram brings up the three Halvering wagons and the ore eleven days back, and spending somebody else's money. Hask is quiet for a moment, which for Hask is a long time. He watches the iron colour.

"Who told him what, I couldn't say. Half that table would tell you their mother's weight for a free ale." He pulls the bar, looks at it, puts it back. "Nobody sold to him. Nobody had any to sell. But the second night I went home and he was still sitting there. Him and Taerom, heads together at the end of the bench, and Taerom wasn't laughing." He shrugs his huge shoulders. "Taerom had bar coming up that lot. The first lot. The one that came in two wagons short." *Clang.* "Make what you like of that. I'm not saying anything. I'm saying who was sitting where."

By the open end, Tam has gone very still with his arms still folded. He doesn't speak, but his eyes have left Hask and moved to the road outside, the grey line of the Coast Way going south.

Hask draws the bar out once more, sights along it with one eye shut and drops it into the slack tub. Steam goes up between them. When it clears he's looking at Bram, at how he stands outside the spark line with his hands still and his eyes on the work and not on the smith. It's the same blunt horse-dealer look as before, only slower this time.

"You've swept a forge floor," he says. "Don't tell me you haven't. You stand like a man who got clouted for standing anywhere else." He reaches for the next bar. "Whose yard?"
````

## Round 15

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't smile, but something in his face eases, the way a man's does when a stranger names his home town.

"Halloway's yard. Fields of the Dead, outside the Gate, by the north road. My father shod carthorses and mended wagon tyres for the funeral trade. Dead men's carts break like anyone else's." He nods at the slack tub, still steaming. "I got clouted for standing in the spark line, for touching his tongs, and for talking while the iron was colouring. Mostly for that last one. Left at sixteen. He said I'd never make a smith because I watched the road more than the fire. He was right."

He waits while Hask takes up the next bar and it goes into the coals. Then he speaks into the gap the way he did before.

"Taerom. Is he a smith, or a factor? Where would a man find him this morning?" He keeps it mild. "I'm not going to put my fist in his face over who sat where. I'd just like to hear from him what the Amnian wanted to know, and whether he told him." A short pause. "If a man rides his wagons out on the days somebody asked about over free ale, and then those wagons don't come in, he might be glad to talk. Or he might be the last man in Beregost who wants to."

He glances at Tam, who is still watching the road, and adds one more thing in the same level tone.

"And the Amnian. Did he say a name, or where he was going after? South, or back up toward the Gate?"

Then he goes quiet again and watches the iron colour, outside the spark line, and leaves Hask the next stroke.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Hask doesn't look up from the fire while Bram talks. When Bram gets to *dead men's carts break like anyone else's*, the hammer stops for half a beat above the coals before he sets it down on the anvil's heel.

"Funeral trade." He pulls the bar out, decides it isn't ready and pushes it back in. "Huh. Good steady work, that. The dead don't haggle and they never come back to say the tyre's gone loose." He looks sideways at Bram for a moment. "Your old man was right. You stood there the whole time watching my door and my fire both, and you thought I hadn't noticed."

There's no insult after it this time, which for Hask is the same as a compliment.

He keeps an eye on the bar while Bram asks about Taerom, and answers in time with the work.

"Smith." The bar comes out yellow and goes onto the anvil. *Clang.* "Best in Beregost, he'll tell you. The trouble is he's right." *Clang.* "He buys bar off the factors by the wagonload and sells blades and armour south and north both, so call him a factor too if you like, he won't thank you for it." *Clang, clang.* He turns the bar. "Thunderhammer's up the hill, on the left before the temple road. Big stone place, double doors, his name over them in iron letters as long as my arm. He'll be at his anvil now with two journeymen sweating for him. He doesn't like being looked at while he works. He likes being asked questions while he works even less."

Bram mentions the days and the free ale, and Hask snorts into the steam as the bar goes into the tub.

"Here's what I'll say about Taerom. He told the whole of Beregost he lost two wagons. Stood in the market and shouted it. He never told anybody he'd sat with that Amnian." He wipes his wrist across his forehead. "Maybe there was nothing to tell. You'll find out how glad he is to talk."

Over by the open end, Tam speaks without turning his head from the road. "Ambrel leaves Nashkel first day of every tenday. Has for years." He takes a moment over it. "Anybody on the Way knows that, Halloway. Nobody needed to buy them ale for it."

Hask grunts. He can't argue with that, and he doesn't like it.

"The Amnian. Name." He thinks about it with the next bar in the tongs, and his stubbled brows draw together. "Never gave me one. Or he did and I was drinking. Ask Orla, he slept under her roof." He puts the bar into the coals. "Going after? Said Nashkel, for the fair. Every Amnian on this road says Nashkel. That's where Amn starts." He shrugs his huge shoulders. "He went south. That's all I saw. I saw his back going out the door."

The bar heats. The boy works the bellows, and the wet rag on his forearm flaps with every pull. Hask stands a moment with his hand on the tongs, looking at the two cold hearths and not at anybody. Then he reaches up to a rack of tools above the anvil, frowns at it, and holds the long tongs out to Bram handle first. It's the first courtesy he has shown anyone this morning.

"Funeral-cart Halloway. Hold that bar in the fire for me while I find the swage some idiot's put back in the wrong place." He doesn't look at the boy. "Don't let it go grey on me."
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Good. Follow the brother’s advice,” I tell the apprentice, and leave him to his rhythm.

I step back beside Tam, out of the way of both Bram and the work. I listen while Bram has his say, keeping the sack marks fixed in memory for the road.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa says it quietly enough that it stays under the roar of the hearth. "Good. Follow the brother's advice."

The boy goes red again, all the way to his ears, and nods without breaking his count. "Yes, mistress." He gives the bellows a pull that's harder than it needs to be, the rag flaps, and he catches himself before Hask can see it.

Hask has heard her anyway. He doesn't turn around. "He'll follow mine first, or he'll follow it out the door." He goes on digging through the rack over the anvil with the long tongs still held out behind him, handle first, toward the man in mail. They don't move from where they are. They're still waiting for Bram.

Sefa steps back onto the packed earth beside Tam, well clear of the spark line and of whatever Bram does next. The heat from the one live hearth reaches this far as a dry pressure on her face. Tam shifts half a step to give her room against the post. He keeps his arms folded and his eyes on the grey line of the Coast Way outside the open end. He doesn't say anything for a while. Then, low, under the bellows:

"Your quiet one. With the bag." He doesn't look round. "Not at the temple. Not here." A pause while he works out how to put it. "Still riding tomorrow, Marrin? Or is it three of us and you."

At the anvil, Hask has found a swage that isn't the one he wants. He swears at it and goes on looking. The bar in the fire is turning from orange to yellow, and nobody has picked up the tongs yet.
````

## Round 16

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “I haven’t seen him since supper,” I answer Tam quietly. “I can’t promise he’ll ride with us. We’ll ask if he comes back to the inn.”

I glance toward him. “But I’m still coming. Nettle’s reserved, and I’ll have her deposit ready tomorrow.”

Then I settle back against the post. “Until he tells us otherwise, best plan for four.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa keeps her voice low, under the roar of the bellows, and Tam hears her out without turning his head from the road.

He takes his time over it, as he does with everything. Out past the open end of the forge a carter's wagon rolls north over the bridge, its load roped under canvas. Tam watches it all the way across.

"Four." He says it as if he's laying the number down on a board to see whether it holds. "Var and me came up from Nashkel with four guards on five wagons, and we lost one." He moves his shoulders against the post. "Four with no wagons is different. No load to watch. Only each other."

He's quiet for a moment.

"Two watches a night, then, not three. Two on and two down, back to back like Halloway said." Then, still to the road: "If he comes back to the inn, he'll find us in the yard at first light. We won't wait on him."

That settles it as far as Tam's concerned. He doesn't ask anything else about the man with the bag.

At the anvil Hask has pulled a second swage off the rack, held it up to the light from the open end, and sworn at it more quietly than at the first. That probably means it's nearly the right one. He hasn't looked round. The long tongs are still out behind him, handle first, toward the man in mail by the door. In the hearth the bar has gone from yellow toward the pale straw colour that comes just before iron starts to spit. The apprentice has noticed. He keeps pumping, looks at the bar, then at Bram, then back at the bar, and says nothing at all.

*(Out of game: Bram, the tongs are still yours to take or not.)*
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram takes the tongs without a word. He doesn't make a show of it, and he doesn't grin. He sets his shield against the post by the door, out of the way, and steps up to the hearth on the side away from the bellows, so he isn't in the boy's road. He gets a proper grip low on the handles, with his weight back and his elbows in, the way he was clouted into holding them at twelve, and he lays the bar in the heart of the coals.

He keeps it there and turns it a quarter now and then so it heats even. He watches the colour come up the iron and lets the forge be quiet for a while. Talking while the iron colours was what earned the clout.

When Hask comes back with the swage, Bram draws the bar out at a good yellow and holds it out to him handle-first in the tongs. Then he steps back outside the spark line and picks up his shield.

"Didn't let it go grey." It's the nearest thing to a joke he's made all morning.

While the next blows land, he speaks into the gap, same level tone as before.

"One last thing and I'm out of your forge, master smith. When Taerom's two wagons went, did his drivers go with them, like Brodder's man? Or did anybody come back to tell it?" He waits for a stroke. "And did anyone say the fires burned low that night, or the dogs were quiet? Drivers talk in the market same as smiths."

Whatever Hask says, Bram nods his thanks at the end of it.

"Obliged. If your sacks are out on that road, we'll tell you where we found them." He glances at the cold hearths. "Can't promise they'll be in a state to sell."

Out on the Way, he speaks quietly to Tam and Sefa.

"Thunderhammer next, while he's at his anvil and doesn't want to be looked at. A man who doesn't want questions while he works sometimes answers short just to be rid of you. Then Orla for the Amnian's name. She'll have taken it if she took his coin. Then Anselm after noon." He tips his head at Tam. "You're right that anybody knows Ambrel's days. But the Halvering factor's days and Taerom's might not be so common, and he was asking how many wagons, not when. Somebody was counting guards." His jaw sets a little. "Twelve guards for six wagons on my run, and they let us by. I'd like to know if that's luck, or arithmetic."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't say anything when he takes the tongs. He props the shield against the post by the open end and steps up to the hearth on the far side from the bellows, out of the boy's way. He takes the handles low, weight back, elbows in. The bar is already pale straw at the tip, hotter than Hask left it. He turns it a quarter in the coals so it heats even, and after that the only sounds are the bellows and Hask swearing at his tool rack.

The apprentice watches him for three pulls. Then he stops watching and goes back to counting.

Hask comes back with the right swage at last and drops it into the anvil's square hole. Bram has the bar out before he can turn round. It comes out bright, with no spit of sparks off the end, and he offers it across handle first. Hask takes it without a word and looks along it with one eye shut. Then he grunts, and the grunt has nothing sour in it. Bram steps back outside the spark line and picks up his shield.

"Didn't let it go grey."

"No." *Clang.* Hask has the bar in the swage and is rounding it in his four-beat. "Your old man clouted you right." *Clang, clang.*

Bram asks his last question into the gaps between strokes: whether Taerom's drivers went with the wagons, whether anyone came back to tell it, and whether there was talk of low fires or quiet dogs.

"Both drivers. Gone with the wagons." *Clang.* "Taerom stood in the market and shouted about his iron. Price per bar, the lot. I never heard him shout once about the drivers." *Clang.* "Make what you like of that as well." He turns the bar in the swage. "Dogs, fires, I couldn't tell you. Taerom hired the guards, so ask Taerom. One of them drank at the Wizard after. Ambrel's lot weren't in yet. He said he'd never slept so sound on the road in his life. Said it for a laugh." *Clang, clang.* "Nobody laughed. Two men were gone."

When Bram thanks him, he doesn't look up, and when Bram promises word of the sacks he only nods at the iron. Bram looks at the cold hearths and says he can't promise what state they'll be in. That gets a snort.

"Ore's ore. Rain doesn't hurt it. It just bleeds." He lifts the bar out of the swage. "Funeral-cart. Next time you're standing about idle, stand about in here."

---

Out on the Way the air is cooler, and the noise of the forge falls behind them. A carter's wagon rumbles past toward the bridge. Bram keeps his voice low and lays out the morning: Thunderhammer while Taerom is at his anvil and wants rid of them, then Orla for the Amnian's name, then Anselm after noon. Then he gets to the guards and the counting.

Tam hears him out with his eyes on the road south. He walks a few steps before answering.

"Twelve for six." He says it like a figure on a slate. "Ambrel had four for five. Taerom's lot, I don't know." His jaw shifts. "If somebody's counting, Halloway, they counted us and saw it was worth it."

He looks up the hill, where the roofs climb toward the temple and a big stone building stands on the left before the temple road.

"I'll come up. Taerom won't know my face." A pause. "That might help."

The hammers of Beregost are going all over town now. Thunderhammer is a ten-minute climb.
````

````text
Bram Halloway: Bram nods once at Tam's arithmetic.

"Worth it, or easy. Four for five is easy." He settles the shield on his arm. "Come up, then. You stand by the door and say nothing, and if he looks at you, let him wonder."

They start up the hill. Halfway up he glances at Sefa.

"Here's how I'd play Taerom, unless either of you has better. Hask says he hates questions at the anvil. So we don't ask about the Amnian first. We ask about his drivers. A man who shouted the price of his iron in the market and never once named his drivers has a sore spot there. Press on that and see which way he flinches." His mouth tightens. "If he snaps at us to get out, fine. A short answer tells you things too. If he's frightened, he'll ask *us* what we know. Then we've got him."

He thinks a few steps more.

"And I'll ask who he hired for guards and how many. The one who slept sound at the Wizard. If that man's still in Beregost, I'd like a cup with him before we ride."

At the double doors with the iron letters, Bram stops, reads the name, and lets the heat and the two journeymen's hammers wash over him for a moment. He doesn't step in until there's a break in the striking. Then he goes a pace inside, stands outside the spark line like he did at Hask's, shield low, hands still, and waits for the master smith to look up.

When Taerom does, Bram speaks plainly and keeps it short.

"Master Taerom. Bram Halloway, caravan guard up from Nashkel. I won't keep you from the iron long." He lets a stroke fall. "We ride for the old waystone tomorrow, where Ambrel's tail wagon went. Your two drivers went the same way, I hear, with your wagons. I'd like their names, and the name of whoever you had guarding them. If they're out there, I'd sooner know who I'm looking for."

Then he stops talking and watches the man's hands on the hammer.
````

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Insight (Wisdom +1): reading Taerom's hands at the word "drivers" · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Insight (Wisdom +1): reading Taerom's hands at the word "drivers" · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam hears out *worth it, or easy* without breaking stride. "Easy," he says after a dozen steps, like a man accepting a bill he can't argue with. When Bram tells him to stand by the door and let the smith wonder, one corner of his broad face shifts. "I'm good at that."

The climb is steeper than the one to the temple. The cobbles run up between smiths' yards, and every yard has its own hammer going. Halfway up, Bram lays out how he'd play Taerom: drivers first, the Amnian later, a short answer is still an answer, and if Taerom gets frightened he'll start asking them questions. Tam listens with his eyes on the street ahead. At the part about the guard who slept sound, he grunts.

"Guards that sleep don't get hired twice. He'll still be in town. Nobody's paying him to leave."

Bram's question to Sefa, whether she has a better way, goes up the hill with them unanswered.

Thunderhammer is where Hask said it would be, on the left before the temple road. It's a big building of grey stone with a slate roof and double doors of iron-strapped oak propped open with a pair of anvils. Above them, TAEROM is spelled out in wrought iron, each letter as long as a man's arm, and every curl of them is clean. Nothing in this yard was made in a hurry.

Inside, the heat hits harder than it did at Hask's. There's one big hearth with a hood of riveted plate over it, and it's going white at the heart. Two journeymen in leather aprons stand at the anvil with sledges, striking in turn, *one-two, one-two*, on a thick billet. A third man leads them with a light hammer, tapping the spot where he wants the next blow, and they put it there every time. Along the walls hang finished blades, a rack of spearheads and a breastplate on a wooden stand, polished bright enough to throw the firelight back.

Bram stops at the doors and lets the noise wash over him. When the light hammer rings twice on the anvil's face, the sledges stop, and he steps a pace inside. He stands outside the spark line with his shield low and his hands still. Tam takes the doorpost behind him and folds his arms.

The man with the light hammer looks up.

Taerom is no Hask. He's tall and long in the arm, grey-bearded and trimmed close, and his apron is clean. Hask would call that an insult. His hands are big and scarred, but he holds the hammer loosely, like a pen. His eyes go over Bram once: the mail, the painted shield, the way he's standing. Then they move to Tam at the door, stay there a moment, and come back.

Bram keeps it short. He gives his name and his trade. He's riding for the old waystone tomorrow, where Ambrel's tail wagon went. Taerom's two drivers went the same way, he's heard. He'd like their names, and the name of whoever was guarding them.

He lets one stroke's worth of silence fall and watches the man's hands.

At the word *drivers*, the light hammer stops a finger's width above the billet and stays there. The journeymen notice. One of them looks at the other.

Bram can't read what's under it. The hammer comes down onto the anvil's heel and Taerom's hand closes on the haft, knuckles pale, and from where Bram stands it looks like plain anger: a busy master who's been interrupted, and by a stranger saying the one thing the whole town has stopped saying to his face. If there's anything else under it, it stays hidden.

"Back to the fire," Taerom tells the journeymen. He doesn't raise his voice. They lift the billet in the tongs between them and carry it to the hearth without a word, and one of them starts working the bellows hard enough to keep himself out of the conversation.

Taerom wipes the hammer face on his apron. It's clean already.

"Wil Arndt and Pedder Lusk." He says the names flat and fast. "Pedder drove for me eleven years. Wil was my wife's cousin. Second season on the box." He puts the hammer down on the bench, squared up with the edge. "Guards were four out of the Friendly Arm. Garra Fell had them. She took her money and rode north the day after they came in. She won't be back here." A short pause. "The one you'll have heard about is Dob Herrold. He drinks at the Jovial Juggler on the Way, as long as somebody's paying. Nobody's paying him to guard anything now."

He gives Tam another look, longer this time, the kind a man gives a face he's trying to place. Then he turns back to Bram.

"You said *the same way*." His voice is still level, but the words come out clipped. "Ambrel's wagon and mine. Who told you that? Hask?" He doesn't wait for an answer to that one. "What do you know about where they went, caravan guard, that you came up my hill to ask me for names?"

Over at the hearth the bellows wheeze. The billet goes from red to orange, and nobody touches the sledges.

 Bram watches Taerom's hands and comes away with nothing beyond the anger. His read of the man has failed: whatever the word *drivers* touched in the master smith, Bram can't tell whether it was grief, guilt or fear. All he's sure of is that Taerom wants him out of the forge.

Taerom is still waiting for his answer. Who told him *the same way*, and what does a caravan guard know about where the wagons went?
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: Sefa's lines belong back at Hask's forge and on the Way, so I've put them there. Now she's at Thunderhammer with Bram and Tam.)*

At Hask's forge, the bar in the coals had gone pale straw. Sefa called it out over the bellows from outside the spark line: "Hask, the bar's going pale." Bram was already moving for the tongs. Hask didn't turn around from his tool rack. "I've eyes in my back, girl. Why d'you think I gave him the tongs?" A moment later Bram drew the bar out bright, with no spitting off the end, and Hask said nothing more about it.

While the next blows rang, Sefa spoke to Tam beside the post: "First light. I'll be there."

Tam kept his eyes on the road. "First light, Marrin." It was the same as saying it was settled.

Out on the Way she waited until the forge noise was behind them, then asked which way to Thunderhammer. She wanted to hear about the other missing wagons before Anselm. Tam lifted his chin toward the hill and the big grey building on the left before the temple road. "Up there. The one with the name on it." She climbed with them and heard Bram lay out how he meant to play Taerom. When he asked if either of them had a better way, she didn't offer one.

---

Now she's standing just inside the iron-strapped doors of Thunderhammer, near Tam's doorpost and clear of the work. The heat from the big hearth presses on her face harder than Hask's did. Along the walls the polished breastplate and the racked spearheads throw the firelight back. At the hearth one journeyman works the bellows hard, and the billet goes from orange toward yellow. Nobody touches the sledges.

She has just heard what she came up the hill for. Pedder Lusk drove for Taerom for eleven years, and Wil Arndt was his wife's cousin. The guards were four out of the Friendly Arm under Garra Fell, who has gone north. Dob Herrold drinks at the Jovial Juggler, as long as somebody's paying.

Taerom's eyes leave Bram for a moment and go to her. He takes in the pack, the road dust and the plain hospice knife at her belt, and he looks at her as if she's one more stranger in his forge than he counted. He doesn't speak to her. He looks back at Bram, and his hand rests flat on the bench beside the hammer he squared up a moment ago.

He's still waiting. Who told the caravan guard *the same way*, and what does he know about where the wagons went?
````

## Round 18

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: I leave Bram to answer Taerom’s question.

When there’s a pause, I say, “Sefa Marrin. I’m going with them to look. If we find Lusk or Arndt, is there anything we should know to help them—an old injury, an illness that needs tending?”

Then I ask, “And how would we recognise your iron if it’s been taken off the wagons? Does it carry your mark, or the supplier’s?”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't answer straight away, and that leaves a gap. Sefa speaks into it, just loud enough to carry over the bellows.

"Sefa Marrin. I'm going with them to look." Taerom's eyes come back to her. "If we find Lusk or Arndt, is there anything we should know to help them? An old injury, an illness that needs tending?"

At the hearth the journeyman's arm falters on the bellows for one stroke, then picks up again.

Taerom doesn't answer at once. It's the first question in his forge this morning that he didn't expect, and for a moment his face shows only that. The cold in it doesn't go anywhere.

"Pedder's knee. The left one." He says it flat and fast, like the names. "A wheel went over it nine years back. He drives sitting and walks on a stick. A mile on rough ground and he's done." He stops, then gives the rest as a plain fact, because that's what it is. "If he walked off that road, he didn't walk far."

"Wil's twenty-two. Nothing wrong with him." He pauses. "He'll talk. Too much. If he's alive, you'll hear him before you see him."

He doesn't say anything else about either man.

Sefa asks her second question, about the iron and whose mark is on it, and Taerom's answer comes quicker. This is ground he likes.

"The supplier's. I put my mark on finished work, never on stock." He nods at the wall, at the polished breastplate. Low on its rim is a small stamped hammer inside a ring. "That bar came from the Orlen bloomery at Nashkel. Every bar is struck at both ends with a pick and a star. Bundles of ten, bound with hoop iron, and a lead tag on the hoop with the bundle number scratched into it." He looks at her steadily. "Forty bundles on two wagons. If somebody's cut the ends off to lose the stamp, the bars will be short. Two fingers short at each end. A smith would see it across a market."

At the hearth the billet has gone bright yellow. One of the journeymen looks at it, then at his master, and says nothing. Taerom sees him look and lifts one finger without turning round: *wait*. The bellows ease off.

Taerom looks past Sefa to the doorpost, where Tam stands with his arms folded, and studies the broad sunburnt face for a third time. Then he turns back to Bram, and his voice is clipped again.

"So. Caravan guard." He hasn't moved his hand from the bench. "Who told you *the same way*? And what do you know about where they went?"

*(Out of game: Bram, Taerom's question is still yours to answer.)*
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't move. He keeps his hands still on the shield rim and answers in the same level voice, without hurrying.

"Nobody had to tell me, master smith. You told the whole market yourself: two wagons, drivers and all. Ambrel's tail wagon went eleven days back, driver, mules and load. The Halvering house has three wagons of bar and a factor a month gone, and they've written to the Wizard asking after him. That's three lots on one stretch of road, and no men coming home from any of them." He lets a moment pass. "I've walked that road sixteen years. That's not three bad lucks. That's one hand."

He tips his head a fraction toward the bellows and the billet going orange. It's a courtesy: *I know I'm costing you heat.*

"What I know, I'll tell you, since you asked straight. Ambrel's wagon went off the Way east of the old waystone. The ruts run a hundred feet and stop on shale. The fire burned low that night with dry wood on it, nobody woke, and the mules never made a sound." His eyes stay on Taerom's face. "One of your guards told the Wizard he never slept so sound on the road in his life. I'd like to hear from Herrold whether his fire burned low too."

He stops there, then adds quietly, with no edge in it:

"I'm not here to lay anything at your door. Your wife's cousin is out there somewhere, and so is Pedder. If we find them, or what's left of them, I'd sooner bring them back to someone who'll say their names. If there's anything else about that run that you didn't shout in the market, a day they were asked about, a man who wanted to know how many guards rode with them, I'd take it as a kindness. And it stays with me."

Then he waits. He doesn't fill the silence, and he watches the hands.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Taerom hears the whole of it with his hand flat on the bench. He doesn't interrupt. When Bram says *one hand*, his jaw shifts under the trimmed beard, but that's all.

At the hearth the billet has gone from orange to yellow. The journeyman on the bellows glances at it, then at his master's back. He keeps pumping and says nothing.

Bram tells him about the shale and the ruts, the fire burning low with dry wood on it, and the mules that never made a sound. Taerom's eyes leave Bram's face for the first time. They go to the white heart of his own fire and stay there a moment, as if he's measuring it.

"Low," he says. "With wood on it."

He doesn't ask anything more about it. When he speaks again his voice is level, but it's slower than before.

"Fell told me her watch slept. All four. She stood in my yard and said it to my face like it was weather." He takes his hand off the bench. "I paid her what I owed her. Then I told her that if she came up my hill again, I'd have her put off it. That's why she's gone north."

Bram mentions Herrold, and Taerom gives a short nod. "Ask him. He'll tell you for the price of a jug. He told everyone else."

Then Bram gets to the drivers and the names, and to *someone who'll say them*. Taerom's hand comes back to the light hammer on the bench. He nudges it a finger's width until the haft lines up with the edge, though it already did. His eyes are on Bram the whole time.

"Wil's mother comes to my door every morning," he says. "Before my wife is up. She doesn't knock. She stands in the yard until somebody sees her." He waits a moment. "If you find them, you bring word up this hill first. Before the market, before Hask, before anybody. Ten gold for it, living or not. I'll pay it into your hand myself."

He doesn't answer the rest. Bram asked about a day somebody wanted to know, and a man who wanted to know how many guards rode. He gets nothing back on either. The gap where an answer should be lasts one breath, then two, and Taerom lets it stay empty. His hand stays on the squared-up hammer.

"Your heat's burning, master," the journeyman at the hearth says quietly. He sounds apologetic.

"I know what my heat is doing." Taerom doesn't look round. Without hurry, he lifts the light hammer off the bench. "Caravan guard. You've had your names. You've told me your road. Now I have work, and you're standing in my light." He looks past Bram for a moment, to the doorpost. It's a fourth look at Tam's broad, sunburnt face, and it's as short and puzzled as the others. Then he turns away. "Out. Both of you. All three."

The light hammer rings twice on the anvil face. The journeymen bring the billet across between them, yellow and spitting at the edges, and the sledges start up again: *one-two, one-two*. Every blow lands where the light hammer taps. Taerom doesn't look at the door again.

---

Outside under the iron letters the air is cool after the heat. Down the hill, Beregost's hammers are going in every yard. Up the street the temple road climbs toward the Song of the Morning, and the sun stands well clear of the hills now. It's a little after nine. Noon and Anselm's side door are three hours off, and the ride is tomorrow at first light.

Tam comes off the doorpost and falls in beside Bram. He walks a dozen steps down the cobbles before he says anything.

"He looked at me again." He keeps his eyes on the street ahead. "I've never shod a horse in his yard, Halloway. Never sold him a thing." He shrugs his heavy shoulders. "Don't know what he sees."

The morning is yours. Orla has the Amnian's name if anyone wrote it down, Herrold drinks at the Jovial Juggler, and nothing else comes looking for you before noon unless you go looking for it.

*(Out of game: no roll was needed. Bram told Taerom the truth, and Taerom reacted to what he heard.)*
````

## Round 19

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram walks a few steps down the cobbles before he answers. He's turning it over.

"Four looks is too many for a stranger." He keeps his voice low. "Either you've got a face like somebody he knows, or he's seen you somewhere and can't place where." He glances sideways at Tam. "Think back, Brodder. Were you at the Wizard last month, the nights that Amnian was standing rounds? Or in Nashkel when Taerom's lot left? If you were drinking at the next table while the Amnian and Taerom had their heads together, he might remember the back of your neck better than you'd like."

He lets that go. It's a question, not an accusation.

"He gave us one thing he didn't mean to. I said a day asked about, a man asking how many guards rode, and he went quiet and straightened a hammer that was already straight." His jaw sets. "That's no answer, but it's not a denial either. A man with nothing to hide would have told me to go hang. Ten gold for word up his hill first, before the market and before Hask. He wants to hear it before anybody else does. Maybe for the cousin's mother." A shrug. "Maybe not."

He looks back up at the iron letters, then down toward the Way.

"Three hours to noon. Here's my thinking. The Wizard first, it's on the way. Orla for the Amnian's name, if he gave her one, and I'll pay Dannet the deposit on Brick while I'm in the yard, so it's done. Then the Jovial Juggler. Herrold will talk for a jug, and I'd sooner buy it before noon, while he's still got half his wits." He turns his head to take in Sefa as well. "Then the temple at noon, Marrin, if you'll have me at that table. Brodder, Anselm's not your ground, I know. Find Var and tell her what we've got. And if there's anything about that waystone night you haven't said yet, tonight would be the time to say it."

He settles the shield and starts down toward the Coast Way. He takes the street side again and watches the doorways out of habit.

At the Burning Wizard he goes in by the yard. He counts fifteen gold into Dannet's hand for Brick's deposit and hands him the silver bit he promised, now that he's seen the cob stand. Then he goes into the common room to find Orla.

"Mistress. One more question, and I'll not leave you a copper for it, so you'll keep talking." There's a dry glint in his eye. "The Amnian in the good coat, two nights in my room. Did he give you a name when he paid? And did he ask you anything? Who was due up the road, or how many rode with them?"
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Tam walks a dozen steps with the question before he answers. Under them the cobbles fall away toward the Way, and in every yard they pass a hammer is going.

"No." He keeps his eyes on the street. "Start of last month I was in Nashkel. Sat on Ambrel's step most of a tenday waiting to sign. Var with me." A few more steps. "Never been up that hill before this morning. Taerom's lot left before ours. I didn't see it go."

When Bram gets to the hammer that was already straight, Tam grunts, and the grunt agrees with him. He has nothing to say about the ten gold.

He hears out the plan for the morning and nods once at *find Var*. At the last part, about the waystone night and anything he hasn't said yet, he stops walking. A carter's boy with a barrow has to swing round him.

"I've told you all of it, Halloway." His voice is low. "I've told it to myself more times than that. It comes out the same every time." He starts walking again. "If anything else comes back, it'll be tonight."

---

The Burning Wizard's yard smells of wet straw and fresh dung. The morning's mucking out is half done. Ilse Var is in the open stall nearest the pump with a dun gelding's forefoot between her knees, picking the hoof clean. A black mare is tied at the next ring. She looks up when the two men come through the gate. Her eyes go to Tam's face, then to Bram's hands, and back to the hoof.

Tam goes to her and squats beside the dun. He talks too low to carry, a sentence at a time. Ilse keeps working. Halfway through she stops with the pick still and looks across the yard at Bram. Then she nods once and goes back to the hoof.

Dannet comes out of the feed store with a bucket in each hand. He sets them down when he sees Bram counting coin into his palm. He watches all fifteen gold go in and then counts them again himself, one at a time, the way he'd count a stranger's horse's teeth.

"Early. I like early." He drops the coins into a pouch inside the oversized jerkin, takes the chalk off the ledge by the door, and writes PAID in square, downhill capitals under BRAM HALLOWAY on the end stall. Brick doesn't look round from his hay.

Then Bram holds up the silver bit. Dannet looks at it, then at the cob, and shakes his head.

"Said when he walks out. He's stood, that's all you've seen." He picks up his buckets. "Tomorrow, first light. Keep it warm till then."

---

The common room is mid-morning empty. The benches are down off the tables now, and the shutters are open to let the night's smoke out. Orla is behind the counter rubbing down cups with a cloth. She hears Bram's opening and doesn't look up.

"Good. Keep it that way." She sets a cup on the shelf and takes up the next one. "Dask. That's what he gave me at this counter when he took the room. No first name, and I didn't need one. His coin was good."

She takes longer over the second question and rubs at a ring that won't come off.

"Not me. He'd asked the smiths all that, the road and the wagons and who was due. You've heard it from Hask by now, I'd guess." Another cup goes on the shelf. "What he asked me for was hot water, morning and night, and a second blanket. He paid for both and didn't ask the price. Kept his boots by the door and the room cleaner than he found it." She shrugs. "That's all I've got on him, Halloway. I've had worse in that room."

The common room is quiet behind him. Somebody is knocking a cask in the cellar, and out through the open shutters the Coast Way runs south toward the Jovial Juggler, a few minutes' walk. It's about half past nine, and noon at the side door is two and a half hours off. Nothing else comes up before then unless you go looking for it.

*(Out of game: Sefa, Bram asked whether you'll have him at Anselm's table, and then he and Tam went down the hill. I've left Sefa under the iron letters at Thunderhammer until you say where she goes.)*

 *(Out of game: none of the four needed a roll. All of them were plain questions, a payment or an instruction to Tam, and the story already gave each one's result: Tam was in Nashkel all of last month, Orla had the name Dask and nothing about the road, Dannet took the fifteen gold and chalked PAID, and Tam went over to Var and told her.)*
````
