### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Thank you. If we find Pedder, we’ll make provision to carry him rather than ask him to walk.”

I step back beside Tam, leaving Taerom’s question to Bram. While I listen, I fix the marks in memory: a pick and star at both ends, ten bars to a bundle, numbered lead tags.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: Sefa's line belongs back inside Thunderhammer, straight after Taerom told her about Pedder's knee, so I've put it there.)*

Taerom had just said it flat: *if he walked off that road, he didn't walk far.* Sefa answered him before he could turn back to Bram.

"Thank you. If we find Pedder, we'll make provision to carry him rather than ask him to walk."

Taerom's eyes stayed on her a moment longer than they had before. "He'll hate it," he said. Then, just as flat: "Carry him anyway." That was all he said. He turned back to Bram, and the cold came back into his voice for the rest of it.

Sefa stepped back to the doorpost beside Tam, who shifted his weight half a step to make room for her. She left Taerom's question for Bram. Bram answered it, and Taerom threw them out. While the two men talked, she went over the marks Taerom had given her: a pick and a star struck at both ends of every bar, ten bars to a bundle bound in hoop iron, and a lead tag on each hoop with the bundle number scratched in it. Forty bundles on two wagons. A bar with its ends cut off would be two fingers short at each end.

---

Now she stands under the iron letters outside Thunderhammer, and the cool air is welcome after the heat of the big hearth. Behind her the sledges have started again, *one-two, one-two*, landing wherever the light hammer taps. Down the hill every yard has its own hammer going. Up the street the temple road climbs to the Song of the Morning, where the marigolds will be fully open by now. It's a little after nine.

Bram has laid out his morning where she could hear it. The Wizard first, for the Amnian's name and Brick's deposit. Then the Jovial Juggler, to buy Dob Herrold a jug before noon. Then Anselm's side door. He sent Tam to find Var, and then he looked at her: *Then the temple at noon, Marrin, if you'll have me at that table.* He and Tam are already going down the cobbles toward the Way, Bram on the street side out of habit, and he hasn't looked back for her answer.

*(Out of game: Sefa, Bram's question is still yours, and so is where she spends the next three hours.)*
````

## Round 20

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Of course I’ll have you there,” I call, catching up with Bram. “We’re going down the same road tomorrow. Better we hear the same things today.”

I walk with him toward the Wizard. “I’ll settle Nettle’s deposit while you see to Brick, then come along to hear Dob. If he was there when those wagons vanished, I’d like to ask what he remembers of the night—not just where the tracks went.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: Sefa catches Bram on the cobbles below Thunderhammer, so she's with him in the yard and at the counter.)*

Bram and Tam are only a street down when Sefa calls after them. Bram stops and lets her catch up. Tam doesn't stop, but he slows down.

"Of course I'll have you there. We're going down the same road tomorrow. Better we hear the same things today."

She falls in beside them and tells them her plan for the morning: Nettle's deposit while Bram sees to Brick, then Dob Herrold, and she wants to ask him about the night itself, not only the tracks. Tam listens to that part and grunts. "The night's the part nobody asks," he says, and that's all he says about it.

---

So Sefa is in the Wizard's yard when Dannet counts Bram's fifteen gold twice and chalks PAID. When she holds out her own deposit, his eyebrows go up. She doesn't have fifteen in gold. What she has is five gold and one coin he doesn't see often in a stable yard: a heavy white platinum piece.

Dannet takes it between finger and thumb and holds it up to the light. Then he bites it, carefully, at the edge, like a man who has heard you're supposed to and isn't sure what he's checking for.

"Ten gold, that is." He doesn't make it a question. He counts the five gold in on top, drops the lot into the pouch inside his jerkin and pats it flat. "Fifteen. Square." He goes to Nettle's door and chalks PAID under SEFA MARRIN, in capitals that slope downhill even more than the others. "Morning, she's yours. Fed and brushed, like I said."

Nettle puts her grey nose over the door, smells the chalk and sneezes.

Sefa stands at Bram's shoulder at the counter too, so she hears Orla give the name *Dask* and nothing else: hot water morning and night, a second blanket, a room left cleaner than he found it. Tam stays out in the yard with Ilse and the dun's hooves.

---

The Jovial Juggler is a few minutes south along the Coast Way. It's bigger than the Wizard: two storeys of timber and whitewash with a long porch, and over the door there's a painted sign of a man in motley keeping five red balls in the air. The paint has faded, so two of the balls are almost gone and he seems to be juggling three and a ghost. At this hour the porch is empty apart from a cat. Inside, a pot-boy is sweeping last night's rushes out the back, and the common room smells of stale beer and fresh bread from somewhere behind it.

There's only one customer, and he's easy to find.

He's on the corner bench under the stair with his back to the wall, which Bram notices. He's a big man going soft, about forty, with a week of grey stubble and a good leather jerkin worn over a shirt that has been slept in more than once. A guard's sword hangs in its scabbard from the bench-end, and the buckle is polished even if the man isn't. In front of him is a clay jug on its side, and he's tilting it with one finger to see whether anything is left in it. Nothing is.

He looks up when the door opens. He takes in the mail and the shield, then the young woman with the pack, and his face cheers up like a man seeing rain clouds over a dry field.

"Well, now." His voice is too loud for an empty room. "That's a caravan man if I ever saw one, and he's not come in here for the bread." He rights the empty jug and sets it squarely in front of the seat across from him, like laying a place at table. "If Taerom sent you, I've told him all of it, twice. If Fell sent you, she owes me four silver. And if you're buying" — he grins, and it's a good grin, the kind that has been getting him free drinks for twenty years — "then sit down, friend, and I'll tell you about the best night's sleep I ever had."

At the counter the pot-boy leans on his broom. "Jug's two silver," he says to nobody in particular. "Morning price."

Dob Herrold waits with his hand on the empty jug and his eyes, which are sharper than the rest of him, on Bram's face.
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods to Orla. Whatever he thinks of the name, his face doesn't show it.

"Dask. Hot water and a second blanket, and the room left cleaner than he found it." He says it back to her the way Tam does, fixing it in place. "That's a man used to being looked after, or used to leaving nothing behind. Thank you, mistress." True to his word, he leaves no copper. He just taps the counter twice with a knuckle and turns away.

On his way out through the yard he stops by Ilse's stall. He doesn't crowd her or the dun.

"Var. Jovial Juggler, looking for Dob Herrold. One of Taerom's guards, the one who slept sound. If you've a mind to hear what he says about his fire, you're welcome. If not, I'll tell it tonight." He waits long enough for a nod or a shake, then goes either way.

---

At the Jovial Juggler he takes a look at the room from the door before he goes in: who's drinking at this hour, where they sit, and which one is the man still nursing last night. Then he goes to the counter.

"Dob Herrold. Is he in?" He sets a silver down. "And a jug of whatever he drinks, if he is."

If he's pointed toward him, Bram carries the jug over and sets it on the board in front of the man. He sits down across from him, side-on, with his back toward the wall if there's wall to be had and the shield leaning against the bench.

"Bram Halloway. Caravan guard up from Nashkel. That's yours, and there's no hurry on it." He pours the first cup himself and pushes it over. "I'm riding for the old waystone tomorrow, where Ambrel lost his tail wagon. You rode Taerom's run with Garra Fell. Tell me about that night. Not the laugh part, the real part. How did the fire burn? Did you put wood on it? Did the dogs or the mules make any sound? And before you left Nashkel, did anyone stand you a drink and ask how many of you were riding?"

He keeps his voice easy, one road man to another, and doesn't push. He watches the man's face and hands while he drinks.
````

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Insight, Wisdom +1: reading Dob Herrold's face and hands while he tells the night · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Insight, Wisdom +1: reading Dob Herrold's face and hands while he tells the night · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram says it back to Orla the way Tam does, to fix it in place. "Dask. Hot water, a second blanket, and the room left cleaner than he found it." Then he taps the counter twice with a knuckle and leaves no copper. Orla looks at the knuckle, then at him, and goes back to her cups. One corner of her mouth moves a little.

On the way out through the yard he stops at Ilse's stall, far enough off to give her and the dun room. He tells her where he's going and who he's going to see, and that she's welcome to come.

Ilse lowers the dun's hoof. She looks at Tam beside her, not at Bram, then shakes her head once. "Horses," she says. Tam doesn't look up from the girth strap he's checking. "Tell it tonight, Halloway."

---

The Jovial Juggler looks the way it did a minute ago when Bram looked in from the door. A cat sits on the porch. The pot-boy is leaning on his broom. Dob Herrold is on the corner bench under the stair with his back to the wall and an empty jug set in front of the empty seat.

Bram goes to the counter anyway, because that's where you ask. "Dob Herrold. Is he in?" From the corner Dob lifts one hand and waggles the fingers. Bram puts one silver on the board. "And a jug of whatever he drinks."

The pot-boy looks at the single coin and then at Bram. "Two, the jug. That's half." He doesn't argue the point any further. He takes the jug from in front of Dob, fills it to the shoulder from the cask under the counter, and sets it down. It's exactly half full.

Dob watches it come across the room in Bram's hand and laughs. It's a big laugh for such an empty room.

"Half a jug. Friend, that's the most honest thing anybody's bought me this tenday. A man who pays for half wants half the story, and I can do half sober." He shifts along the bench so his sword stays at his own end. His eyes are on the girl with the pack as she takes the place beside Bram, and his face cheers up a little more.

Bram sits side-on with the wall at his shoulder and leans the shield against the bench. He gives his name and his trade and pours the first cup himself. Then he asks it plainly. He's riding for the waystone tomorrow, and he wants the real part of the night, not the part Dob tells for a laugh. How the fire burned, whether anyone put wood on, whether the dogs or the mules made a sound, and whether anybody in Nashkel stood him a drink to ask how many were riding.

Dob drinks half the cup before he answers. While he talks, Bram watches his hands and his face.

"Waystone. Same as everybody. It's the only spring between Lionshead and the crossing worth stopping for." He sets the cup down. "There were four wagons. Taerom's two rode at the back, because iron's heavy and heavy rides last. Fell had first watch, I had second, and Kettering and the boy had third and fourth."

"The fire." He pauses there long enough to mean it. "I put an armful on at the change. Good dry stuff, a big armful, because it was cold. In the morning that armful was still sitting on it, hardly scorched, and in the middle of it there was a red eye about the size of my palm. No ash to speak of. It looked like it had been burning all night without eating anything."

"Dogs. Fell had a brindle bitch, a big soft thing that barks at her own tail. When I woke up, sun well up, she was lying across Fell's boots, and when Fell kicked her she didn't want to get up. Nor did Fell." His grin comes back, but it's thinner. "Mules on the other two wagons were stood there hipshot, quiet as cows. Taerom's teams were gone with the wagons. The ruts went east off the road and quit on the stone. Fell walked it an hour and came back with nothing, swearing."

He drinks again and takes his time over the last question.

"Nashkel? Nobody bought me a drink to ask how many. Fell did all the hiring talk. People don't stand Dob a drink to ask him questions. They stand him one so he'll stop talking." He laughs at that himself. "I sat my watch. Back to a wagon wheel, second hour, eyes open. Then it's sunup and Fell's kicking her dog."

He says it in the same easy voice he used for the rest. He's told it often enough that it comes out in the same order every time. Bram can't tell whether the jokes are covering something or whether that's just how the man talks. He can't find a place where the story catches, and he can't swear there isn't one.

Dob looks over at the girl with the pack, then back at the half jug, which is a good deal less than half now.

"You're riding tomorrow." His eyes have gone sharp again, sharper than the rest of him. "Short a sword? I'm out of work, I've slept at that waystone, and this time I'd quite like to see what does it."

 Bram watches Dob's hands on the cup and his face over the rim the whole time he talks, looking for the place where the story catches, and he doesn't find it. He comes away with nothing he'd trust. He can't tell whether the jokes are there to cover a lie or a shame, or whether that's just how the man talks. Whatever Dob may be holding back about his own watch, it gets past Bram.
````

````text
"Waystone. Same as everybody. It's the only spring between Lionshead and the crossing worth stopping for." He sets the cup down. "There were four wagons. Taerom's two rode at the back, because iron's heavy and heavy rides last. Fell had first watch, I had second, and Kettering and the boy had third and fourth."

"The fire." He pauses there long enough to mean it. "I put an armful on at the change. Good dry stuff, a big armful, because it was cold. In the morning that armful was still sitting on it, hardly scorched, and in the middle of it there was a red eye about the size of my palm. No ash to speak of. It looked like it had been burning all night without eating anything."

"Dogs. Fell had a brindle bitch, a big soft thing that barks at her own tail. When I woke up, sun well up, she was lying across Fell's boots, and when Fell kicked her she didn't want to get up. Nor did Fell." His grin comes back, but it's thinner. "Mules on the other two wagons were stood there hipshot, quiet as cows. Taerom's teams were gone with the wagons. The ruts went east off the road and quit on the stone. Fell walked it an hour and came back with nothing, swearing."

He drinks again and takes his time over the last question.

"Nashkel? Nobody bought me a drink to ask how many. Fell did all the hiring talk. People don't stand Dob a drink to ask him questions. They stand him one so he'll stop talking." He laughs at that himself. "I sat my watch. Back to a wagon wheel, second hour, eyes open. Then it's sunup and Fell's kicking her dog."

He says it in the same easy voice he used for the rest. He's told it often enough that it comes out in the same order every time. ## Round 21
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't answer the offer straight away. He tops up Dob's cup from what's left in the jug and sets the jug down with a little still in it.

"Back to a wheel, second hour, eyes open. Then it's sunup." He says it back slowly, the way Tam does. "No yawning first? No head going heavy? A man who drops off on watch usually knows he's fighting it. He catches himself nodding once or twice before it takes him." He turns his own cup a quarter turn on the board. "Anything just before it went? A smell on the air, a sound out in the scrub, a light up on the hill side? Even something you put down to tiredness at the time."

He lets Dob answer that in his own time. Then he gets to the offer.

"As for a sword, it's not my ride to hire for. It's Brodder's. He lost Ambrel's tail wagon at that same spring, and he's going back for his driver, not for pay. Nobody at that fire's getting paid." He looks Dob in the eye, level. "And I'll be plain with you, since you were plain with me. Every man who slept at that waystone is a man I'd like near me out there. Every man who slept at that waystone is also a man I'd keep an eye on. It's nothing against you. It's the same for Brodder, and he knows it."

He picks up the shield and stands.

"Burning Wizard, tonight, after supper. Brodder and Var will be there. Tell it to him the way you told it to me, the red eye in the fire and the dog that wouldn't get up, and let him look at you while you do it. If he says yes, you ride. If he says no, I'll stand you the other half of that jug for your trouble."

He nods to the pot-boy on the way out, and steps out onto the Way. He checks the sun, figures about two hours to noon, and turns uphill toward the Song of the Morning without hurrying.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't answer the offer yet. He tips the jug over Dob's cup and fills it. A little is still sloshing in the bottom when he sets the jug back on the board. Dob notices that, and his grin twitches.

"Back to a wheel, second hour, eyes open. Then it's sunup." Bram says it back slowly. Then he asks about the yawning, the heavy head, the nod you catch yourself in before it takes you. He turns his own cup a quarter turn and asks about a smell on the air, a sound out in the scrub, a light on the hill.

Dob drinks before he answers. When he puts the cup down, the grin has gone thin.

"No." He says it plainly, and for once it isn't loud. "That's what sticks. I've nodded on more watches than you've had hot dinners, Halloway. Everybody has. Your chin drops, you jerk up, you swear at yourself, you stand up and walk about. I know how that goes." He shakes his head. "Not that night. I don't remember fighting it. I don't remember anything coming on."

He looks at the cup for a moment.

"Smell, nothing. Sound, nothing. I wasn't looking at the hill. You look at the dark past the wagons, not uphill." He stops, frowns and goes on more slowly. "Only this. The fire'd gone small and I thought, I'll feed it in a minute. And I wasn't cold. A cold night, I'd been stamping my feet all through Fell's watch. And I sat there not cold, thinking, in a minute." He shrugs his heavy shoulders. "Then Fell's kicking her dog."

The grin comes back, but it takes some work. "There. That's the bit I don't tell for a laugh."

Bram gets to the offer. It's Brodder's ride and nobody's paid. Every man who slept at that spring is a man he'd want near him out there, and a man he'd keep an eye on. Dob hears it out with his eyes on Bram's face. At *keep an eye on*, he laughs, the big laugh again, and it fills the empty room.

"Fair. I'd watch me too." He lifts the cup to him. "Wizard, tonight, after supper. I'll tell your Brodder about the red eye and the dog, and he can look at me as long as he likes. I've been looked at by worse." He taps the jug with a fingernail. "And if he says no, I'll hold you to the other half."

Bram picks up his shield and stands. He nods to the pot-boy on his way past, and the pot-boy nods back over his broom.

---

Out on the Coast Way, the sun is well up and warm on the back of his neck. By its height it's ten o'clock or near it, so noon is about two hours off. He turns uphill. It's the same climb as at dawn, but the street is full now. A carter is backing a dray into a yard, a girl is beating a rug over a sill, and every smithy along the way is ringing. Thunderhammer's double doors stand open as he passes, and the sledges are still going *one-two* inside. Nobody looks out.

At the top, the gate of the Song of the Morning stands open on an empty yard. The marigolds are fully open now, and the half-empty pail is gone from inside the side door. Someone has moved it. Somewhere inside, a single voice is reading aloud, too low to make out. The side door under the bell-tower is shut, with a bell-pull hanging beside it.

He's nearly two hours early. Nothing calls him here before noon unless he rings.

*(Out of game: Sefa, you're still on the corner bench at the Juggler with Dob and his refilled cup. Bram has gone, and your question about the night is yours to ask.)*
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Sefa Marrin.” I take a seat, leaving the place opposite Dob for Bram. “Taerom told us where to find you. We’re heading south to look for the missing people.”

I rest my hands on the table. “Tell us about that sleep. When did you turn in, and what’s the last thing you remember before it? Anything ordinary is useful too—supper, changing the watch, someone tending the fire.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: Sefa's introduction belongs back at the moment she sat down beside Bram. Her question comes now, after Bram has gone up the hill.)*

When Sefa sat down, the empty place across from Dob stayed free for Bram, as she meant. She gave her name and said Taerom had sent them. Dob had looked pleased then, and he looks pleased again now that she speaks to him directly. The door has just swung shut behind Bram's shield. The pot-boy is sweeping rushes again, and the cat has come in off the porch to sit in the sun by the threshold.

Sefa rests her hands on the table and asks about the ordinary things: when he turned in, supper, the change of watch, who tended the fire.

Dob is surprised enough to laugh. "Supper. Nobody's ever asked me about supper." He tips what's left in the jug into his cup and looks at it fondly. "Beans and bacon out of Fell's pot. All of us ate out of it, drivers too. Kettering cooked, and he burns everything, so it was burnt. We filled the kettles at the spring. Good water, cold, sweet. Every lot on that road drinks it."

He counts the rest off on thick fingers.

"I turned in straight after, under the third wagon. Fell had first watch. Her dog lay by the fire. Taerom's two were back at the tail with their wagons. Pedder slept up on his box, because the knee won't let him crawl under. Young Wil was still talking when I dropped off, something about a girl in Nashkel, and Fell told him to shut it twice." The grin comes and goes. "Fell woke me at the change with her boot, same as always. I went to the woodpile Kettering had cut and took the big armful. Fire was good then. Fell went to her blanket."

He drinks.

"Then I walked the wagons once. Then—" His left hand comes up and pats the breast of the jerkin flat, once, like a man checking he still has his purse, and drops back to the table. "Then I sat down. Back to the wheel. And the rest you've heard."

He says it smoothly, but Sefa catches the seam. He'd been counting the night off step by step, the boot, the woodpile, the walk round the wagons. Then there's a stretch he steps right over, and the hand goes to his chest while he does it. When he looks up from the cup, his eyes go to her hands on the table first and only then to her face. They're sharper than the rest of him, and for a moment they're watching to see whether she noticed.

"Anything else, mistress?" He's loud and easy again. "I've a little left in this, and I'll tell you about Kettering's beans as long as it lasts."
````

## Round 22

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “One thing,” I say, keeping my voice quiet and matter-of-fact. “You remember the change of watch very clearly, then passed quickly from walking the wagons to sitting by the wheel. What happened in between?”

I leave my hands where they are. “If there’s something you’d rather not say across a common room, say so. We’re trying to find Pedder and Wil, not settle your wages with Fell.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Dob doesn't answer straight away. He picks up the cup, finds there's very little left in it, and puts it down again without drinking. Over by the back door the pot-boy's broom keeps scraping. The cat in the sunny patch by the threshold stretches out one leg and settles again.

"Wages with Fell," he says, and laughs, but it comes out quiet. "She'd be owed, not me, if it came to that."

He looks at her hands on the table, then at her face, and this time his eyes stay there. Then his left hand goes back to the breast of the jerkin. It doesn't pat this time. He unlaces the top of it, reaches inside and brings out a flask. It's small and pewter, dented at one shoulder, with a leather-wrapped stopper. He sets it on the board between them, next to the empty jug, and takes his hand off it as if it were hot.

"Brandy. Amnian muck, the cheap stuff they sell at the fair." He keeps his voice down, though there's nobody to hear but the cat. "I walked the wagons once, like I said. Then I took two pulls. Three, maybe. Against the cold, I told myself, and it was cold. Then I sat down with my back to the wheel." He rubs his stubble with the flat of his hand. "That's the bit. That's the bit I walk over every time I tell it."

He turns the empty jug a little with one finger.

"I've stood watch on worse than three pulls, mistress. Twenty years of it. But I keep thinking it was me. The fire went small and I never fed it, and I wasn't cold. Then two men and two wagons are gone off my hour." He doesn't look at the flask. "I tell myself Fell slept too, and she won't touch the stuff. And the dog doesn't drink. I tell myself that most nights. Most nights it works."

Something of the old grin tries to come back and doesn't get far.

"That's all of it. I haven't opened it since. Carried it, though." He nods at the pewter. "Don't know why. To remind me, I suppose."

Then he looks at her properly, sharp-eyed and sober.

"Tonight. Your Brodder, with his watch that went the same way. Does he need to hear this part from me? Or is it enough that you've heard it?"

 *(Out of game: Sefa's question needed no roll. She asked it plainly and kindly, and Dob, who had already been half caught, told her the rest himself: the flask and the three pulls. That's all in the scene above.)*
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't ring. Two hours early is two hours early, and Anselm said noon.

He steps just inside the gate and sits on the low wall by the marigolds, where the old woman sat this morning to be asked how she was. He's out of the way of anyone coming up the hill. He leans the shield against the stone beside him, draws the longsword halfway, and looks along the edge in the sun. He's in no hurry. If it wants a stone, he gives it a few slow strokes. If it doesn't, he slides it home.

If Sefa is with him, he speaks low, so it doesn't carry to the reading voice inside.

"Herrold wasn't cold. Cold night, stamping his feet all through the first watch, and then he's sitting there warm, thinking *in a minute*." He turns that over. "A fire that burns small and eats nothing, and a man who's warm beside it anyway. That's not wet wood and it's not tiredness. Somebody laid a blanket over that whole camp that nobody could see." He glances at her. "You've sat by more sickbeds than I have. Ever see a draught or a smoke do that? Or is it spellwork, start to finish?"

After that he goes quiet and lets the yard be.

If Teo comes out of the side door with a pail or a broom, Bram lifts two fingers to him.

"Morning again, lad. We're early for the Brother, so don't trouble him. If there's water wants hauling while I sit here, I know where the well is now."

Otherwise he stays on the wall and watches the gate and the street below. Every so often he looks at the bell-tower shadow to judge how near noon it is. He doesn't get up until the shadow says it's time, or until somebody comes out to fetch them.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't ring.

He steps just inside the gate and sits on the low wall by the marigolds, where the old woman sat this morning. The stone is warm already. From here he's clear of anyone coming up the hill, and he can see down the cobbles as far as the first turn. He leans the shield against the wall beside him and draws the longsword halfway.

In this light the edge is easy to read. He tilts it and runs his eye down it from the guard. There's a small bright scuff a hand's width from the tip, an old one, and it hasn't turned the edge. Nothing on the blade needs a stone. He slides it home, and the yard is quiet again apart from the reading voice inside the nave, rising and falling too low to make out.

The side door under the bell-tower opens after a while. Teo comes out backwards, dragging a broom nearly as tall as he is, and stops when he sees who's on the wall.

Bram lifts two fingers. "Morning again, lad. We're early for the Brother, so don't trouble him. If there's water wants hauling while I sit here, I know where the well is now."

Teo looks at the gate as though he expects more strangers behind Bram. Then he looks at the pots.

"He's reading to the two novices. He does till the noon bell." He leans on the broom, which makes him look more like Dannet than he'd want to know. "The marigolds want water before the sun gets on them, sir. It's my job, but if you're..." He doesn't finish it. "The pail's back on its hook. Someone moved it inside the door this morning and I fell over it."

That pail is on the hook behind the tower, just as he says. The windlass sticks halfway again and gives when Bram leans on it. He carries the water round and goes along the row of clay pots while Teo sweeps behind him. Teo shows him with one hand: at the root, not on the flowers. Twelve pots take two pails. Teo sweeps the yard from the gate to the steps in long, careful strokes and says nothing else. When Bram sets the empty pail down by the side door, the boy nods to him, a little less shy of the mail than he was at dawn, and goes back inside with the broom.

After that the yard is Bram's. Down the hill the hammers go on in every yard. A carter goes past the gate leading a lame horse and doesn't look in. A woman with a basket comes up as far as the turn, thinks better of the climb and goes back down. Out of habit Bram watches the street the way he'd watch a road. Nobody else comes. The bell-tower's shadow pulls in across the flagstones toward the foot of the tower, and the marigolds, watered at the root, hold their heads up in the full sun.

By the time the shadow is nearly under the tower, the reading voice inside has stopped. It's a few minutes short of noon. The side door is shut, and the bell-pull hangs beside it.

*(Out of game: Sefa is still at the Juggler, so Bram's question about draughts and spellwork wasn't asked. It can wait until she's beside him.)*
````

````text
By the time the shadow is nearly under the tower, the reading voice inside has stopped. It's a few minutes short of noon. The side door is shut, and the bell-pull hangs beside it.

*(Out of game: Sefa is still at the Juggler, so your question about draughts and spellwork wasn't asked. It can wait until she's beside you.)*
````

## Round 23

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram stands when the reading voice stops. He picks up the shield and settles it on his arm, then walks to the gate and looks down the cobbles for Sefa. It was her hour to set, and he'd rather she was at that table than hear it from him afterwards.

He stays at the gate until the noon bell. If she comes up the hill before it rings, he lifts two fingers to her and waits for her to reach him.

"Herrold wasn't cold," he says quietly as she comes up. "Cold night, stamping his feet all through first watch, and then he's sitting warm by a fire that's eating nothing, thinking *in a minute*. I'll ask you about that later. Draught, smoke or spellwork. You've seen more sickbeds than I have."

If the bell rings and the street is still empty, he doesn't make the priest wait. He goes to the side door and pulls the bell-pull once, not hard.

When the door opens, whoever it is, he dips his head.

"Bram Halloway, for Brother Anselm at noon. Marrin set the hour. She's been kept down the hill and may be along." He looks at whoever opened it. "If the Brother would rather wait for her, I'll sit on the wall again. If not, I'll hear it and carry it to her word for word."

Then he waits on the step with his hands still on the shield rim.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The reading voice inside the nave stops in the middle of a sentence and doesn't start again. Bram gets up off the wall, settles the shield on his arm and goes to the gate.

The cobbles below are empty. The heat has come up off the stones, and the forge smoke from the lower town hangs flat over the roofs. Every yard still has a hammer going. A girl crosses the first turn with a basket and doesn't look up the hill. There's no pack and no road dust. Sefa doesn't come.

Behind him the side door opens and Teo comes out at a trot. He doesn't look at the gate. He goes to the foot of the tower and takes the rope, glances once at the sun, which is standing almost straight overhead, and pulls. The noon bell is lighter than the dawn bell, three strokes, quick and plain, the way a household rings for dinner. Down the hill a dog answers it. Teo hangs the rope back on its cleat and goes in again, and the side door shuts behind him.

The street is still empty.

Bram crosses to the side door and pulls the bell-pull once, not hard. Somewhere inside a small bell answers.

Teo opens the door. He's out of breath from the stair, and he's already tucking his robe-sleeves back down over his wrists.

Bram dips his head. "Bram Halloway, for Brother Anselm at noon. Marrin set the hour. She's been kept down the hill and may be along." He looks at the boy. "If the Brother would rather wait for her, I'll sit on the wall again. If not, I'll hear it and carry it to her word for word."

Teo opens his mouth to answer, then thinks better of answering for his master. "He said you'd ring. Wait, please, sir." He leaves the door open and goes off up a narrow stone stair inside, two steps at a time.

Through the doorway Bram can see the foot of the bell-tower from inside: a whitewashed passage, the rope's lower end coming down through a hole in the boards, a row of pegs with two rose-coloured cloaks on them, and the half-empty pail on its hook just inside, where Teo hung it. It smells of beeswax and cold stone.

Anselm comes down the stair after a little. He has changed out of the dew-dark vestments into a plain rose robe with the sleeves pushed back, and there's ink on the side of his right forefinger. He looks past Bram at the gate, sees the empty yard, and then looks back at him.

"Kept down the hill." It isn't a question. He glances up toward the sun, out of habit, though from the passage there's nothing to see but the yard wall. "Come in out of the heat, Master Halloway. I'll not make you sit on a wall twice in one day."

He stands aside. Past him, off the passage, a door stands open on a small room under the tower. It has one window facing east, a scrubbed table, two stools and a bench, and a shelf of ledgers. In the middle of the table lies a folded letter, weighted at one corner with a smooth grey stone. The paper is creased soft from handling. Beside it are a jug of water and two cups.

"Here are my terms," Anselm says, and his voice is as even as it was on the steps. "The letter is on the table, and you may read it now. It's Master Fenwick's, and it's no secret. The other matter I'd rather tell once, to both of you, with the door shut." He pauses. "I'll give her until the shadow comes off the sill. A quarter of an hour, perhaps. If she isn't here by then, I'll tell it to you, and you'll carry it to her as you said."

He waits in the passage with one hand on the door, for Bram to come in or not.
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “It’s enough that you’ve told us. Thank you.”

I glance at the flask, then back at him. “Drinking on watch was a risk. But Tam was walking his line, not sitting down, and his fire went small too—with dry wood still on it. His camp slept through a wagon and two mules leaving. That doesn’t prove what happened to you, but it’s reason enough to keep looking beyond the brandy.”

I pause. “When your fire went small, do you remember whether it still had unburnt wood on it? And did Fell’s dog seem quite itself when you woke?”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Dob lets out a breath when she says it's enough. He has been holding it a while, and he doesn't hide that it was there. He looks at the flask on the board between them, picks it up, turns it once in his thick fingers and tucks it back inside the jerkin. He laces the top shut over it.

"Enough." He nods slowly, as if he's trying the word for size. "Thank you, mistress." His eyes are still sharp, but there's no grin behind them now. When she gets to Tam walking his line and his fire going small all the same, he looks at her for a long moment. "Walking. Not sat down." He rubs his stubble. "Nobody told me that part. I only heard Ambrel lost a wagon."

He gives her questions more thought than he gave Bram's. Maybe it's because he isn't performing now.

"The wood, yes. Like I said, the armful was still sat on top of it, hardly scorched, with that red eye in the middle." He frowns at the table. "There's something I didn't say, because I didn't think of it till now. No smoke. A fire that size, with green ends in the pile, should smoke all night and get in your eyes. When Fell had me up and I looked at it, it was just sitting there, and the air over it was clear as glass."

He stops on the dog and thinks properly.

"Slow. She lay across Fell's boots like a sack, and when she got up she walked like a pup that's had a knock on the head. Wouldn't eat. By the time we'd yoked the teams she was herself again, though, nosing round the mules and getting kicked for it." He shrugs his heavy shoulders. "Fell was the same. Thick-headed till near noon. She said it was like coming up out of a fever."

Then he puts his hand flat on his chest, over the laced jerkin, and his mouth twists.

"That's what I didn't like, now you ask. Three pulls of that muck and I'm usually sorry for it next morning. Head like a dropped anvil." He looks at her. "I woke clear. Clearest I've woken in a year. Fell's the one who doesn't drink, and she was the one with the thick head."

He sits back against the wall and turns the empty jug a little with one finger. The pot-boy's broom scrapes along the back passage. The cat on the threshold has moved after the sun and lies stretched out half across the doorway.

"If you want any more of the night, I've told it all now. Honest this time." Some of the old ease is creeping back into his voice. "I'll be at the Wizard after supper, like I told your caravan man."

It's about ten to eleven. Bram has gone up the hill, and Anselm's side door is at noon. The rest of the morning is Sefa's to spend however she likes.
````

## Round 24

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “The clear air over the fire is worth knowing. So is the difference between how you and Fell woke. I’ll pass that on.”

I rise from the bench. “One last practical thing: where exactly did you camp? Tam lost his wagon at the old waystone south of the crossing. Was yours there too?”

After his answer, I thank him and leave him in peace. I head up toward the temple, turning the accounts over in my mind: fires dimmed without consuming their fuel, sleeping animals, and very different effects on the guards. Does any magic I know of fit those details?

I’ll reach the side door a little before noon and wait for Bram before ringing.
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Arcana: does any magic she knows fit the waystone nights (dimmed fires, sleeping camp and animals, the guards waking differently)? · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Arcana: does any magic she knows fit the waystone nights (dimmed fires, sleeping camp and animals, the guards waking differently)? · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Dob listens to her with his head tilted, the way a man listens to someone reading his own words back to him. When she says she'll pass it on, he nods slowly.

"Do. I'd sooner it went by your mouth than mine. Mine makes a joke of it before I can stop it."

The question about the camp gets a short laugh. It isn't a big one.

"Same stone. Same spring. There's no other place to stop on that stretch, mistress, not with four wagons. You'd be dry camping on the slope." He draws on the board with a thick fingertip, as if the wet ring from the jug were the spring. "The stone stands by the road. The spring's behind it, a stone basin somebody cut a long time ago. There's a flat past that, big enough for a ring of wagons. Fell put the fire in the middle. Taerom's two were on the east side of the ring, toward the hill, because that's the way the road bends, and that's how they came in." He wipes the ring away with the side of his hand. "If Brodder's lot camped there, they camped on the same flat. Everybody does."

She thanks him and gets up. He lifts the empty cup to her from the corner bench with his back still to the wall.

"Wizard, after supper." He says it to the cup more than to her. "I'll be sober. Mostly."

The cat on the threshold doesn't move for her. She steps over it.

---

Out on the Coast Way the sun has come round onto the fronts of the houses, and the hammers are going in every yard. Sefa turns uphill. She takes the same cobbles as at dawn, past the wheelwright's, past Thunderhammer's propped doors and the sledges inside going *one-two*. While she climbs she goes back over everything she has heard. Fires gone small with the fuel still on them. No smoke. Dogs and mules silent, then groggy. A sober woman waking as if out of a fever, and a drinking man waking clear. A watchman who walked his line all night and still lost a wagon. She sets each piece against what she knows of spellwork.

By the time the street turns up toward the temple road, she has worked it through, and she's sure of her ground. The common answer doesn't fit.

The spell every hedge-mage at the Gate calls *sleep* is a short thing. She has seen it cast once, in the hospice yard, on a man who had to be held still for the surgeon. It took him in a breath and let him go inside a minute, and he woke the moment anyone shook him. It takes a handful of people, the weakest first, and a big man or a strong one shrugs it off. It doesn't last the night. It doesn't take eight people, a dog and four mules. And it does nothing at all to a fire.

A draught is a better fit for the people. Put it in a pot that everyone ate from and you get a whole camp asleep, a dog that won't eat in the morning, and a sober woman who wakes thick-headed because she isn't used to anything in her blood. A draught could explain a great deal. It doesn't explain Tam walking his line at Ambrel's camp with nothing in him. And no draught ever made dry wood sit on a fire all night without catching, or took the smoke out of green ends.

That's the part she keeps coming back to. The sleep might be more than one thing. The fire can't be. A fire that gives off no smoke, eats nothing and still keeps a red eye burning till morning isn't a sleeping man's mistake. Something was working on the fire itself, or on the air around it, or on the whole flat by that spring. Something else was working on the people, or the same thing was. Whatever it was, it lay over the whole camp, and it lasted until sunrise. None of the spells she knows would last a night or cover that much ground. Something that did both would be far stronger than anything she has seen cast, or it would be a different kind of thing: something set down there, or something that lives there.

She can't put a name to it. She knows which names it isn't.

---

The noon bell has gone by the time she comes up the last of the cobbles. Three quick strokes, plain as a dinner bell. She hears them from the turn, and a dog down the hill answers them. The gate of the Song of the Morning stands open on the swept yard. The marigolds along the wall have their heads up, and the dark wet rings around their roots show someone watered them not long ago.

The side door under the bell-tower is open. Bram stands on its step with his shield on his arm. Inside, in the whitewashed passage, Brother Anselm has his hand on the door of a small room. He has changed into a plain rose robe with the sleeves pushed back. There's ink on his finger.

Anselm sees her over Bram's shoulder before Bram turns. His eyes go to her face, then to the yard behind her and the sun overhead.

"Mistress Marrin." His voice is as level as it was at dawn. "You're inside my quarter hour. Then I'll tell it once." He stands a little further aside from the door. Past him there's a scrubbed table, and a folded letter on it weighted with a grey stone. "Come in, both of you, and shut the door behind you."

*(Out of game: Bram's scene had the street empty at the noon bell, so Sefa gets to the gate a few minutes after it instead of before. Anselm hasn't started yet.)*

 Sefa got there on her own, between Thunderhammer's doors and the temple road. She set the waystone nights against everything she knew of spellwork, and her learning held up. She ruled out the common spell and the sleeping draught, and she knew why each one failed. That left the one thing she was sure of: whatever was done at that spring worked on the fire itself and lay over the whole flat until sunrise. She couldn't name it. She had worked out which names it wasn't, and she brought that up the hill with her to Anselm's table.
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram dips his head again and steps in. He takes the shield off his arm and leans it against the wall just inside the door, out of the way, where his hand will still find it.

"Fair terms, Brother. Thank you."

He doesn't sit straight away. He waits for the priest to take his own stool first, then takes the bench, which puts his back to the wall and the door and the window in view without making a point of it. He lifts the grey stone off the letter, sets it down beside it so he can put it back exactly, and reads.

He reads it slowly, twice, the way he read the Halvering letter. His lips move a little over names and dates. On the second pass he stops on anything that tells him *when*: when Fenwick wrote it, where from, and what day he meant to leave.

When he's done he folds it back along its soft creases, lays it where it was and puts the stone back on its corner.

"He wrote ahead, then." He keeps his voice low, as if the room were a camp at night. "Most factors don't bother writing to a priest. They write to the house." He looks up at Anselm. "I'll ask one thing about this while we wait, Brother, if it's no part of the other matter. Who else would have known he was carrying it? The house, you, Fenwick. Anyone in Nashkel? Anyone here?"

He pours water for the priest first, then for himself, and drinks.

Then he settles on the bench with his forearms on his knees and watches the shadow on the sill. Now and then he turns his head toward the open passage and listens for boots on the cobbles outside.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: the last scene showed Bram still on the step when Sefa came up the hill. That was my mistake. He'd already gone in, so she finds him on the bench with the letter.)*

Bram dips his head and steps in. The shield comes off his arm and goes against the whitewash just inside the door, where his hand will find it.

"Fair terms, Brother. Thank you."

Anselm takes the stool nearest the window. Bram waits until he's sitting, then takes the bench. From there his back is to the wall, and he can see the door, the passage and the window. He lifts the grey stone off the letter, sets it down beside the paper and unfolds the sheet.

The hand is a merchant's: quick, slanting and clear, with every figure written out in full.

*Brother Anselm,*

*As you asked, a line before I set out. I write from the Belching Dragon in Nashkel, on the last night of the tenday. We roll at first light. Three wagons of the house's bar, Orlen stamp, my own two drivers and four guards I have taken on here. If the road is kind I lie at the Burning Wizard the fourth night and come to your door the morning after. Within the tenday in any case.*

*The thing the house wrote to you of is with me under the house seal, and it will stay so until it is in your hands.*

*Forgive the hand. The lamp here has burned poorly two nights running, and the girl swears the oil is new.*

*Your servant,*
*J. Fenwick*

Bram reads it twice. On the second pass he slows over *last night of the tenday* and *first light*. There's no month and no day number, only the tenday. Anselm said this morning that it came by the post rider a little more than three weeks ago. Put together, that means Fenwick left Nashkel on the first day of a tenday, close to a month back. *Fourth night* means Fenwick planned to camp at the waystone on the third, the same as everyone else. He reads *Orlen stamp* twice. Taerom used those words this morning: a pick and a star at both ends of every bar.

He folds the sheet back along its soft creases, lays it where it was and sets the stone back on its corner.

"He wrote ahead, then." He keeps his voice low. "Most factors don't bother writing to a priest. They write to the house." He looks up. "I'll ask one thing about this while we wait, Brother, if it's no part of the other matter. Who else would have known he was carrying it? The house, you, Fenwick. Anyone in Nashkel? Anyone here?"

Anselm doesn't answer straight away. Bram pours water for him first, then for himself. The priest nods at the cup but leaves it where it is.

"He wrote because I asked him to," he says. "The house told me in the spring who would carry it and roughly when. I sent a line to Nashkel asking him to tell me the day he set out, so I'd know when to start worrying." His mouth moves a little. "I started on time."

He folds his hands on the table.

"Here, I've told no one. Teo knew a factor was due and that I was anxious. He doesn't know there was anything but iron. In Nashkel I can't tell you. Fenwick wouldn't have talked. Whoever packed it for him, whoever sealed it, whoever saw him put a small case in his own saddlebag and not on a wagon, I don't know." He stops for a moment. "The house will know who handled it at their end. I didn't think to ask that. I should have."

That's as far as he goes. His eyes go once to the folded letter under its stone, to the line about the lamp, and then away again. He says nothing about it.

Bram drinks and settles on the bench with his forearms on his knees. A bar of sun lies across the sill, and the shadow of the window frame is creeping toward its edge. The passage outside is quiet. The bell rope hangs still through its hole in the boards.

Then there are footsteps on the cobbles beyond the gate, light and quick with a pack on the back. Anselm hears them a breath before Bram does. He rises, goes to the passage door, looks out across the yard and speaks to the young woman coming in under the noon sun.

"Mistress Marrin. You're inside my quarter hour. Then I'll tell it once." He stands aside from the doorway. "Come in, both of you, and shut the door behind you."

The letter lies on the table under its stone. Bram is on the bench, the second stool is empty, and the door is still open.
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
*(Out of game: the last scene showed you still on the step when the young woman came up the hill. That was a mistake. You'd already gone in, so she finds you on the bench with the letter.)*

You dip your head and step in. The shield comes off your arm and goes against the whitewash just inside the door, where your hand will find it.

"Fair terms, Brother. Thank you."

Anselm takes the stool nearest the window. You wait until he's sitting, then take the bench. From there your back is to the wall, and you can see the door, the passage and the window. You lift the grey stone off the letter, set it down beside the paper and unfold the sheet.

The hand is a merchant's: quick, slanting and clear, with every figure written out in full.

*Brother Anselm,*

*As you asked, a line before I set out. I write from the Belching Dragon in Nashkel, on the last night of the tenday. We roll at first light. Three wagons of the house's bar, Orlen stamp, my own two drivers and four guards I have taken on here. If the road is kind I lie at the Burning Wizard the fourth night and come to your door the morning after. Within the tenday in any case.*

*The thing the house wrote to you of is with me under the house seal, and it will stay so until it is in your hands.*

*Forgive the hand. The lamp here has burned poorly two nights running, and the girl swears the oil is new.*

*Your servant,*
*J. Fenwick*

You read it twice. On the second pass you slow over *last night of the tenday* and *first light*. There's no month and no day number, only the tenday. Anselm said this morning that it came by the post rider a little more than three weeks ago. Put together, that means Fenwick left Nashkel on the first day of a tenday, close to a month back. *Fourth night* means Fenwick planned to camp at the waystone on the third, the same as everyone else. You read *Orlen stamp* twice. Taerom used those words this morning: a pick and a star at both ends of every bar.

You fold the sheet back along its soft creases, lay it where it was and set the stone back on its corner.

"He wrote ahead, then." You keep your voice low. "Most factors don't bother writing to a priest. They write to the house." You look up. "I'll ask one thing about this while we wait, Brother, if it's no part of the other matter. Who else would have known he was carrying it? The house, you, Fenwick. Anyone in Nashkel? Anyone here?"

Anselm doesn't answer straight away. You pour water for him first, then for yourself. The priest nods at the cup but leaves it where it is.

"He wrote because I asked him to," he says. "The house told me in the spring who would carry it and roughly when. I sent a line to Nashkel asking him to tell me the day he set out, so I'd know when to start worrying." His mouth moves a little. "I started on time."

He folds his hands on the table.

"Here, I've told no one. Teo knew a factor was due and that I was anxious. He doesn't know there was anything but iron. In Nashkel I can't tell you. Fenwick wouldn't have talked. Whoever packed it for him, whoever sealed it, whoever saw him put a small case in his own saddlebag and not on a wagon, I don't know." He stops for a moment. "The house will know who handled it at their end. I didn't think to ask that. I should have."

That's as far as he goes. His eyes go once to the folded letter under its stone, to the line about the lamp, and then away again. He says nothing about it.

You drink and settle on the bench with your forearms on your knees. A bar of sun lies across the sill, and the shadow of the window frame is creeping toward its edge. The passage outside is quiet. The bell rope hangs still through its hole in the boards.

Then there are footsteps on the cobbles beyond the gate, light and quick with a pack on the back. Anselm hears them a breath before you do. He rises, goes to the passage door, looks out across the yard and speaks to the young woman coming in under the noon sun.

"Mistress Marrin. You're inside my quarter hour. Then I'll tell it once." He stands aside from the doorway. "Come in, both of you, and shut the door behind you."

The letter lies on the table under its stone. You are on the bench, the second stool is empty, and the door is still open.
````

## Round 25

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 25 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram gets up when Sefa comes in. He nods her to the empty stool and doesn't sit back down until she's taken it. Then he steps past her and shuts the door. He's careful about it so it doesn't bang, and he checks that the latch has dropped before he goes back to the bench.

He slides the letter across the table to her, stone and all, and taps one line with a thick finger. It's the one about the lamp.

"Read that last," he says, low. "*Burned poorly two nights running, and the girl swears the oil is new.*" He looks across at Anselm, not at her. "Your eyes went to it, Brother. Mine did too, after this morning. Fires that burn small and eat nothing on the road. A lamp that burns small in a Nashkel inn, two nights before he rolls." He leaves it there. It isn't a question yet.

"Fenwick wrote it on the last night of the tenday, at the Belching Dragon. He rolled at first light, and he meant to be at the Wizard on the fourth night. So he'd have camped at the waystone on the third, same as Brodder and Herrold." He sits back against the wall. "If it was following him, it was following him before he ever left town."

Then he folds his hands on his knees and keeps quiet.

"Your quarter hour, Brother. We're both here, and the door's shut. Tell it once and I'll not interrupt."

He watches Anselm's hands while the priest talks, and he listens.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa comes in out of the noon glare. Bram gets up off the bench and nods her to the empty stool, then stays on his feet while the stool waits for her. He goes past her to the door and closes it carefully so it doesn't bang. The latch drops into its keeper with a small iron click. He tries it once, then goes back to the bench with his back to the wall.

Anselm stays standing until the door is shut. Then he sits on the stool by the window, and the bar of sun from the sill falls across his hands.

Bram slides the letter across the table toward Sefa's place, grey stone and all, and lays a thick finger on the last lines. He keeps his voice down. He reads the line about the lamp aloud: *burned poorly two nights running, and the girl swears the oil is new.* Then he looks at the priest. "Your eyes went to it, Brother. Mine did too, after this morning." He goes over the dates: the last night of the tenday, first light, the Wizard on the fourth night, so the waystone on the third. "If it was following him, it was following him before he ever left town." After that he folds his hands on his knees and gives the room back. "Your quarter hour, Brother. We're both here, and the door's shut. Tell it once and I'll not interrupt."

Anselm doesn't answer straight away. He looks at the line under Bram's finger the way a man looks at a sum he has already added up several times.

"It went to it," he says. "The first time I read that letter I thought what you're thinking. The second time I told myself the Belching Dragon buys cheap oil and always has. I've read it a good many times since then, and I've come down on neither side." He rests his folded hands on the table. "Fenwick wrote that the case *will stay so*. He was a careful man. I can't tell you whether he kept his word in that room, or whether the girl's oil was bad. I'd sooner you knew I don't know than had me guess for you."

He gets up, takes a ledger from the shelf, a narrow one bound in limp brown leather, and sits down again with it closed in his lap. A folded sheet sticks out of it a finger's width. The wax on it is the same red as on Orla's letter, cut cleanly with a knife, and the device is a ship's lamp in a ring.

"In the spring the house wrote to me. This is that letter. Most of it concerns the house and this temple: what they'll give the Song for keeping a thing, and for how long. That part isn't mine to read out. The rest I'll read to you exactly as it's written, so you have the house's words and not my memory of them."

He unfolds the sheet. The hand is the same small, upright clerk's hand that wrote to the Burning Wizard. He reads in his reader's voice and gives every word the same weight.

"*…an object taken out of a working at Nashkel, which the house will not keep and will not sell. It will come to you under our seal, by the hand of our factor Jory Fenwick, on his first run up the Way after he has word from us. We ask that the Song keep it where the dawn reaches it, and that no one handle it. Do not open the case. When it was opened in our presence, every flame in the room burned low, and stayed low until it was shut again. There are those who have asked to buy it. Refuse them, whoever they say they are.*"

He folds the sheet along its creases and puts it back in the ledger, leaving the same finger's width showing.

"*A working.*" He says the word again and turns it over. "I took it to mean a mine: a gallery, a cut, a place where men dig. Nashkel is all mines. It may mean something else. I didn't write back to ask, and I didn't ask who the buyers were. I was asked to keep a thing behind the dawn and not to look at it. That's the kind of thing a temple says yes to."

Bram watches his hands through all of it. They hold the ledger steady while he reads. They stay steady at *flames*, and at *do not open*. Once, at *whoever they say they are*, the thumb presses flat on the leather and stays there until the sentence is done. Then it lifts. That's the only thing they do the whole time. It isn't the hand of a man inventing anything. He's reading words that frighten him a little, and he has read them often enough to know which ones.

"That's all I was told," Anselm says. "The house knows more. Whoever opened it in front of them knows more. I know what I've read you, and I know the man who was carrying it has been a month on the road and never came to my door." He sets the ledger on the table beside Fenwick's letter, so the two lie side by side under the window. "You asked me this morning on the step whether it was a thing a man should be careful carrying. Now you have everything I had to answer you with."

The bar of sun has crept off the edge of the sill. Outside the shut door the passage is quiet, and the bell rope hangs still in its hole. A long way down the hill the hammers are still going.

*(Out of game: Sefa, the stool is yours if you want it. Anselm has said everything he came to say.)*
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 25 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Sefa Marrin: “Thank you for making the time.”

I step inside with Bram and shut the door behind us, then take a place at the table without touching the letter.

“Before we set out tomorrow, we need to know how to handle the case safely. We’ll bring it sealed if we find it. Is there any danger in carrying it—or anything we should do if its seal is already broken?”

I leave the question there and listen.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
*(Out of game: Bram had already latched the door behind her when she came in, so it's shut.)*

Sefa takes the empty stool. Bram's finger has left the lamp line, and Fenwick's letter is still lying on her side of the table under its grey stone. She leaves it where it is and doesn't touch it.

"Thank you for making the time."

Anselm inclines his head. When she asks about carrying the case safely, and what to do if the seal is already broken, he hears the whole question through. He doesn't answer straight away. His eyes go once to the ledger lying beside the letter.

"Closed, I don't believe it does anything. That's what the house wrote, and it's all I can tell you. Nobody who has carried it has complained of it." He pauses. "Except that nobody who has carried it has come to my door, so take that for what it's worth."

He rests his hands flat on the table.

"If the seal is broken, I told Master Halloway this on the step. Wrap it in whatever you have, leave it in its case, and don't look. I'll add one thing, because I've had three weeks to think about that line." He nods at the ledger. "*Every flame in the room burned low.* If it's open and you have it with you overnight, don't keep it by your fire. Put it as far off as the camp allows, and put your fire between it and the people sleeping. I don't know that it makes any difference. But you'll be sleeping at that spring, and you've heard what the fires do there."

He doesn't make anything more of it. He looks east once, through the little window, out of habit.

"And when the sun comes up, let it lie where the light reaches it. The house asked that of me, so it may be no bad thing on the road either." Then, more quietly: "That's the end of what I know, Mistress Marrin. Everything after it is my guessing, and you can guess as well as I can."

He gets up, takes the ledger back to its shelf and lines it up with the others, so the folded sheet still sticks out a finger's width. He leaves Fenwick's letter on the table.

"If you find Master Fenwick, send word up the hill before anything else. If he's alive, I'll come down to him myself." He puts his hand on the latch, but he doesn't lift it yet. "Is there anything else either of you needs from me before you ride?"

Through the window the sun has gone off the sill. It's just after half past twelve. Tonight after supper Dob Herrold will be at the Wizard. Until then, nothing else happens this afternoon unless you want it to.
````
